The Cohen Family Edition
‫סדור זכרון גליקל חנה‬
‫לימות החול‬

In loving memory of

‫גליקל חנה בת חיים וחיה צינה ע”ה‬

Mrs. Gloria Cohen ‫ע”ה‬
‫נפ' כ”ח שבט תשע”ד‬
Thornhill, Canada
The oldest child of survivors of the Shoah, her parents emigrated to
Canada to begin life anew, while retaining the Torah values of their
upbringing.
Named after two grandmothers, she was a bridge to the Torah and
Yiddishkeit that infused the shtetls of Staszow and Kusnica, where
most of the Jewish residents perished al Kiddush Hashem.
Family, Torah and tefillah were her life, and a siddur and Tehillim
were never far from her hands.
She led her life with those eternal principles in mind and made those
values the pillars of her house.
With a kind disposition and a compassionate demeanor, she was a
friend to many, a cherished member of her community, a devoted
wife to her husband, a loving Eema and Bubby to her children and
grandchildren, and is dearly missed by all.

Sara Goldman ‫ע”ה‬

‫כ”ב שבט תשע”ט‬

Li’chvod my Rosh Yeshiva

HaRav Yitzchak Feigenbaum
You have always been an inspiration to me. You personify what a
true Torah Jew is and have shown me by example a love for learning.
I miss your Thursday night shmuzim and still hear your niggunim for
the Rosh Hashanah and Yom Kippur davening in my head every year.
I wish you much mazal and brachah on the completion of this siddur.
May Hashem bless you, your rebbetzin, and your entire family with
good health and happiness until 120.
With much love and gratitude,
AVI AND ILANA TOBIAS

‫ואלו יעמדו על הברכה‬

Mr. Mayer Rosenzweig
Mr. Josh Salmon

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS
If ever there were a situation where one should start their
acknowledgments by thanking Hashem, it is surely when
writing a commentary on a siddur!
Yet, to be honest, I struggle with that. Whenever I see
authors start by offering thanks to Hashem, I’ve always
thought to myself, “Duh! Isn’t that obvious? Does it really
need to be said?” But I now understand that it does need to
be said! (See FAQs for why.) But more importantly, I learned
what I am thanking Him for. At the end of Modim D’Rabbanan,
there is a curious phrase, “Al she’anachnu modim lach,” and I
struggled to understand what it meant. I discovered that we
are thanking Hashem “for instilling in us the concept and
(crucial) character trait of gratitude!” It’s so easy (at least
for some of us) to confidently roll along feeling that we’re in
control — just that we need Hashem to not “get in our way.”
What a tragic mistake. As I look back at all the details Hashem
had to put into place in order to get me to the point of putting
out this siddur, it is humbling indeed! So I do want to offer
shevach v’hodaah l’Hashem Yisbarach, but not simply for giving
me the wherewithal to produce this work. More importantly,
I want to thank Him for giving me the ability to recognize
His Hand in all areas of my life, being able to recognize my
strengths and know my limitations, and most importantly, for
having sense enough to thank Him for all that!
Clearly, without the support, understanding, and binah
yeseirah of my eishes chayil, Chaya Lana, my chinuch career
would never have happened. So many times, I was simply MIA
at crucial moments, yet she kept the ship afloat and allowed

Acknowledgments XII

me to help others. And from experience I have learned how
true it is that our wives are blessed with a binah yeseirah. Her
insights and intuition have proven invaluable time and time
again. She truly knows me better than I know myself and
has helped me keep my balance. Of course, my entire family,
kids, kids-­in-­law, grandkids, and great-­grandkids have been a
huge support in so many ways. And they were always there for
that important constructive criticism that only close family
can offer. My daughter Batya deserves a special shout-­out;
her addition to our family had a profound effect in so many
ways! But regarding this siddur, it is truly her brainchild. It
was her idea, she constantly pushed me forward, and she was
my in-­house expert on all things “teenager.” In the merit of all
the tefillos generated by this siddur, may Hashem answer all
her requests.
“U’mi’talmidai yoser mi’kulam.” Over the last forty-­five years,
I have been blessed with amazing talmidim and talmidos who
have constantly inspired me with their honesty and spurred
me to new levels of understanding by their questions. To all
of you I can truly say, “She’eilas chacham chatzi teshuvah”; your
honesty forced me to be honest! Over the last eighteen years,
my talmidos in Tiferes Bais Yaakov have allowed me to try out
explanations, concepts, and sources and have enabled me to
develop the approach to chinuch and the hashkafah curriculum
that is reflected in this siddur. To all of them, and to all those
who took care of the material needs of Tiferes so that I had
a free hand to develop the ruchniyus, what more can I say
but … thank you!
“Acharon acharon chaviv.” In life, it doesn’t matter what
you say, but rather, what people thought you said! And in
chinuch that is most certainly true! In addition, what we hear
is affected by what we see! From simply taking care of my
poor grammar (we didn’t do grammar in the ’60s!) to taking
complex ideas and ensuring people would “hear” what I “said,”

XIII Acknowledgments

the staff at Mosaica Press have used their extensive, G-d-­given
skills in a most marvelous way! And then they took all that
and turned it into a user-­friendly siddur that is a paradigm
of “zeh Keili v’anveihu.” But I’m not surprised. From my first
meeting with Rabbi Haber and Rabbi Kornbluth, it was
clear that they “got it” (the highest compliment in “teenage-­
ese”). They understood the need, they had vision, and they
are people devoted to helping educate Klal Yisrael. Once I
realized that, it was obvious that the best thing I could do
was to leave this siddur in their good hands. And the results
speak for themselves. Special mention must be made of Mrs.
Gross and Mrs. Broyde. Even more than their expertise (and
their considerable patience with me), it was their vision and
dedication that made every page of this siddur a literary and
aesthetic work of art.
To all those who had a hand in taking this siddur from an
abstract concept to its present form, I bentch you with what we
say three times daily in Shema Koleinu: “U’mi’lefanecha malkeinu
reikam al teshiveinu — Do not turn us away empty-­handed.” In
this siddur we gave the following explanation: Of course, we
hope that Hashem will grant us what we have requested. But
sometimes, for reasons known only to Him, Hashem must say
no. We therefore ask Hashem that if the answer to our tefillah
must be no, then at least He should not send us away feeling
“empty-handed,” i.e., with a feeling of total rejection. Let our
hearts always be filled with emunah that we had the amazing
zechus and opportunity to talk one-­on-­one with Hashem and
that He heard our tefillah — even if the best thing for us right
now is a no.
‫אמן כן יהי רצון‬

INTRODUCTION FOR PARENTS,
MECHANCHIM, AND MECHANCHOS
Introduction for Teens on page XVIII

It was Friday morning at a Torah Umesorah convention.
Principals of girls’ high schools were sitting around a table
(a “networking” breakfast, for those familiar), and someone
posed a question: “We’re having such a problem with davening
in school! Does anyone have any ideas?” And someone
responded, “Is there anyone who is not having a problem with
the davening in school?”
Tefillah is the Achilles’ heel of chinuch — both in school and at
home. One can mandate putting on tefillin, and whether the
boy is engaged with the mitzvah or not, he has nonetheless put
on tefillin! If a girl’s skirt is appropriate, she is tzanua whether
she is wearing it that way out of love for the sanctity of tz’niyus
or because she doesn’t want to get a detention! But mumbling
Hebrew words in order to avoid dirty looks from the “prison
warden” who is wandering up and down the aisles glaring at
people (or from your father sitting next to you) — that’s not
tefillah! It may look like a beautiful davening to the outsider
watching, and you can boast, “See, our students/my children
don’t talk during davening!” But in reality, all it accomplishes is
the creation of PTSD (post-­traumatic siddur disorder). When
that young person sees a siddur later in life, or walks into a shul
as an adult, they’ll have flashbacks and feel anxiety and pain.
Tefillah is “avodah she’ba’lev,” and it is meant to be a very
personal and heartfelt communication with Hashem. The
siddur is supposed to help facilitate that connection. Chazal
wove many deep, meaningful, and lofty ideas and concepts

XV Introduction for Parents, Mechanchim, and Mechanchos

into the tefillos. Much of tefillah is Tehillim, which are certainly
meant to convey many layers of beauty and meaning.
Unfortunately, in today’s world, many young people (and
adults) are not comfortable with Lashon Hakodesh, and even
the English translations do not always “speak” to them; besides
the fact that many phrases defy translation. In addition,
teenagers (and adults — but we try not to let teens know that)
have many complex and sticky questions about the tefillos
and about their day-­to-­day relationship with Hashem. These
questions get stuck in their brain and impede their ability to
talk to Hashem honestly and openly.
So how will this siddur help? This is not a full and literal
translation of all the tefillos. Rather, it is an overview of what I
believe to be the basic main point of each part of the weekday
siddur, written in simple English, using terms that are relevant
to today’s teenagers. The translations, explanations, and
concepts were culled from a wide variety of sources (mefarshei
haTanach, mefarshei tefillah, sifrei mussar, hashkafah, and
chassidus), mixing, matching, and merging — even within the
same pasuk — in order to create a work that is hashkafically
sound, meaningful, and readable. In addition, it deals with
the common questions we all have when we take our davening
and relationship with Hashem seriously. My goal is to ease
the “angst” of these questions, while helping teens (and us!)
understand something of what the tefillos are meant to convey,
thereby making it easier for all of us to pour out our hearts to
Hashem on a daily basis.
Will this solve all tefillah problems? Nope! After spending
months studying the tefillos and writing this commentary,
I still find myself at times taking three steps backward and
saying to myself, “Oh! I guess I just finished Shemoneh Esrei!”
(Sound familiar?) For some people, because of their G-d-­given
nature, davening will always be easy (just like for some people,
because of their nature, they will have an easier time being

Introduction for Parents, Mechanchim, and Mechanchos

XVI

patient or charitable). And for some, because of their G-d-­
given nature, davening will be hard (just like for some people,
because of their nature, they will have a harder time being
patient or charitable). We all have areas of avodas Hashem that
are easier or harder. But if used properly, in conjunction with a
positive atmosphere and healthy chinuch, this siddur will help
your children and students be the best daveners they can be.
And you will learn much in the process!
‫אמן כן יהי רצון‬
Note Regarding This “Translation”

First of all, this is not a literal translation. Nor does it
explain every word or sentence of every tefillah. Many tefillos
have multiple layers of meaning, and at times, a literal
translation makes certain sentences appear repetitious when
we know that in reality, they are not. My goal was to be brief,
convey what I understand to be the essence of the tefillah, and
facilitate a meaningful conversation with Hashem.
Thus at times, long paragraphs have only a few lines of
English (e.g., Hodu and Az Yashir). In addition, you may find it
difficult to see where what I have said is found in the specific
words of a tefillah (especially Tehillim). That is because the
mefarshim and midrashim I used to explain that tefillah add
ideas based on the context of that chapter within the Sefer
Tehillim, and within the context of David HaMelech’s life.
Tefillos that I felt are most central and are rarely skipped
(e.g., Ashrei) I explained in greater detail and I tried to stick
closer to the actual words. But even in such instances, I
added words into the translation in order to make it more
personal, readable, or convey the essence of the meaning as
understood by the mefarshim. To denote that these words are
not found in the text itself, they will be in italics: e.g., “‫ַא ְׁש ֵרי‬
‫יתָך‬
ֶ ‫יֹוׁש ֵבי ֵב‬
ְ — Happy are those who (wherever they are) are living
in the house of Hashem.” That addition in italics is based on

XVII Introduction for Parents, Mechanchim, and Mechanchos

the commentary of Rav Samson Raphael Hirsch, who explains
that with the proper values and mindset, any home, wherever
it is, can become “the house of Hashem” even if it is not
situated in Yerushalayim or the Beis Hamikdash.
In addition, commentaries like the Malbim and Rav Hirsch
stress time and time again that many words in Lashon
Hakodesh have multiple meanings, but those meanings are all
interconnected if one understands the depth behind them.
My choice of translation of certain key words was based on
what I felt fit the meaning in that context, using terms that
teens could relate to, and would most create a sense of real
conversation and connection with Hashem. A few examples of
translations that do not fit the norm:
• ‫ ברוך‬as in ‫ — ברוך אתה ה׳‬You, Hashem, are the Source of
everything, as explained by Derech Hachaim quoting the
Avudraham, Rabbi Munk, and others.
• ‫ קדוש‬as in ‫ — אתה קדוש‬You, Hashem, are separate and
removed from this physical world, as Rashi explains ‫קדושה‬
at the beginning of Parashas Kedoshim.
• ‫ שם‬as in ‫ ישתבח שמך‬or ‫ — ברוך שם‬May the impact of all You
do (‫ )שמך‬be praised; or May the perception of Hashem’s
Presence be increased, as in the pasuk: “‫ — טוב שם משמן טוב‬A
good reputation is better than good oil” (Koheles 7:1). ‫שם‬
refers to reputation or how someone is known.
Throughout this siddur I have used Hebrew words, terms,
and concepts written in Ashkenazic transliteration. In order
not to impede the flow, I chose in general not to translate
them, but I have provided an extensive glossary at the end of
the siddur so that this work will be accessible to all.

INTRODUCTION FOR TEENS
“But I Don’t Do Davening!”

(“OK, I do davening, but I don’t enjoy it!”)
OK, I get that. That is actually the most common comment
made by young people who struggle with davening. “Did you
daven this morning?” “No.” “Why not?” “I dunno.” “I dunno is
not an answer!” “I dunno — I just don’t DO davening.”
So we need to figure out why you don’t do davening, or even
if you do daven, why you find it so hard and not enjoyable.
You’ve heard all the mussar shmoozes, speeches, guest
speakers, Yom Iyuns, workshops, and Biur Tefillah classes.
You’ve read all the books, tried all the workbooks, workshops,
methods, and surefire tricks. You’ve been offered prizes — and
threatened. Yet, for some reason, you look at the siddur and
freeze! Or worse — you open it, try your hardest to daven, and
mess up! Then you feel even more guilty than you did before,
and you’re more convinced than ever that “Hashem certainly
will not answer a mess-­up like me!” Now you’re even further
from your goal! And you do want to daven and enjoy davening
(or at least you want to want to daven and enjoy it). You
see that bachur shokeling away with his face contorted with
emotion, or that girl with her siddur stuck right up to her face
crying, and you wonder, “Why can’t I be like them? What’s the
matter with me?”
But maybe that’s the problem! Perhaps you’re trying to be
them. Perhaps you’re trying to be someone else. That’s not how
tefillah works! ‫ — ואני תפילתי‬my tefillah is a reflection of the real
“me.” As we explain in this siddur, first we proclaim, “‫לי‬-‫זה א‬

XIX Introduction for Teens

‫ — ואנוהו‬This is my G-d (I will create my own personal relationship
with Hashem),” and then we say, “‫ — אלוקי אבי וארממנהו‬He is the
G-d of my father (and that unique relationship will be built on
the foundations of our ancestors).” You need to be yourself — and
being yourself can be quite scary and difficult. If you can’t
learn to have a sincere conversation with yourself, then you’ll
certainly find it difficult to talk to Hashem! So maybe it’s not
the siddur; maybe it’s you! But the more you learn to be open
with yourself, the easier it will be to talk to Hashem! And while
this won’t always be easy, recognizing the issue is half the
battle. And now you have something to ask Hashem: “Please,
Hashem, help me become accepting of myself, able to see my
potential, to recognize what I need to improve, and to have
the courage to ask for help to become the best me possible!”
That is certainly a good starting point!
Perhaps there are other possible reasons you “don’t do
davening.” Perhaps you have questions — Hashem doesn’t
seem fair, He never answers anyway, etc. OK — the FAQs
section in this book addresses those questions. Perhaps you
don’t understand the Hebrew? OK — there are translations.
Perhaps you don’t understand the English? OK — so use this
siddur; it’s easy to understand! Perhaps you’re afraid that if
you actually daven, it will make the adults in your life happy,
which will cause them to start demanding more of you (like
when you suddenly get an 80 instead of your usual 65 and
they respond: “Great! If you can get an 80, then next time you
can get a 90!”). Or maybe — even worse — you’re afraid of your
reputation! You’ve created this persona of a “non-­davener”
and you’re comfortable with that since it is safe and doesn’t
demand much from you (you can’t mess up being a non-­
davener!). If you start davening, what will the chevrah say?
What will all your BFFs say?
OK — I hear that. So don’t “daven” then. Talk to Hashem.
When? On your way to yeshiva or school. In English. No

Introduction for Teens

siddur required — just have a conversation with Him about
your life, what’s going on, and where you could use some help.
(You won’t even look weird — people will assume you have a
Bluetooth!)
I once walked into my ninth-­grade Chumash class when I
was giving a test. I saw a girl mumbling to herself and assumed
that she was reviewing Rashi. When I got closer, I heard she
was saying, “Please, Hashem, let Mashiach come before Rabbi
Feigenbaum gives this test!” I told her that it looked like that
wasn’t going to happen. When her face fell, I suggested she ask
Hashem that Mashiach should come before I grade her test!
Her face lit up and she blurted out, “Oy, Hashem, please let
Mashiach come before Rabbi Feigenbaum grades this test!”
That was a 100 percent true, heartfelt conversation with
Hashem. (Unfortunately for her and for all of us, what she
wanted didn’t happen. So why did Hashem turn down her
request? See the FAQs.)
But I would like to suggest one last possible reason why it
is hard for you to daven. Davening is a middah, like patience,
generosity, social skills, or organization. Hashem creates
people different. Everyone has stuff that is naturally difficult
for them, and they must work really hard to get it right. Take
me: I have poor math skills, sloppy handwriting, and I struggle
with knowing when to stop talking and start listening.
Everybody has things that are easier for them and things that
are harder: Some boys have zitzfleish and can sit for hours
concentrating on a sugya. Others lose their place after a few
seconds — even though they can fix every broken desk in five
minutes! Some girls by their nature love going to visit old
ladies in old age homes. They find it fun — “Oh, they’re so
cute” — and naturally do that chessed. Others find it painful:
Old age homes make them nervous, they don’t know what to
say, and they feel like dorks.

XX

XXI Introduction for Teens

The same is true with davening. Some people, by their
G-d-­given nature, enjoy davening. They were born with good
language skills, and the words speak to them — it’s just their
thing! For others, it’s simply a battle. Just like some kids
always seem to know what to say and the chevrah are always
crowding around them, and others find themselves constantly
in awkward social situations. Or for some girls, tz’niyus comes
naturally, and for others it’s a struggle. That’s how Hashem
created us; we each have our own strengths, weaknesses, likes,
dislikes, and battles.
It is self-­understood that Hashem expects you to do the
best you can — not less. But He also doesn’t expect you to
be someone else! So what to do? I can’t help you with your
school rules or your parents’ expectations. Forget those for a
moment. More important is your relationship with Hashem.
It’s a slow process. That’s OK. Start slow. How about one Asher
Yatzar in the morning. Read it slowly and really think about
how lucky you are that you can go to the bathroom and say
thank you — in English! Take one brachah of the Shemoneh
Esrei each week and stop, read the English explanation, and
think about what it means to you — and tell Hashem what
you’re thinking.
One brachah! All growth is in stages.
Be wary of what the mussar sefarim call the “frum yetzer
hara.” He gets you to jump too fast, too far, too quick. Then
you fall and thus convince yourself that you’re a loser — and
give up! They say that Rav Shach, zt”l, on Rosh Hashanah
accepted upon himself that once a day he would be careful
to say bentching from a siddur rather than by heart. And he
didn’t promise himself to do this forever but until Chanukah!
Something doable so you feel you accomplished something.
Then on to the next step.
Will this be a steady climb up? Nope! Not at all! You’ll have
bad days and crash. We all do. (Remember, an EKG that’s a

Introduction for Teens

XXII

straight and smooth line means you are dead!) I too have
found myself walking backward and saying, “Oh, I guess I just
finished Shemoneh Esrei” — and I wrote this siddur!
The Satmar Rebbe, zy”a, used to say the following: “Eileh
va’rechev v’eileh va’susim” — when the non-­Jew wants to travel,
he needs horses and chariots. But “anachnu b’sheim Hashem
Elokeinu nazkir” — if we want to “travel,” all we need to do is
start davening and our minds are all over the place!
So don’t be afraid to join the club of those who struggle
every day to focus, think seriously, and actually have a heart-­
to-­heart talk with our Creator.
How will this siddur help? This is not a full and literal
translation of all the tefillos. Rather, it is an overview of what I
believe to be the basic main point of each part of the weekday
siddur, written in simple English in order to help you talk to
Hashem. In addition, it deals with the common questions we
all have when we take our davening and relationship with
Hashem seriously.
Of course, whenever possible, you should say the tefillos
in the original Hebrew and use this merely as an aid in
understanding what you are saying. Chazal have infused our
tefillos with many very special meanings. There’s a reason
why it’s called Lashon Hakodesh! But remember, Hashem
understands all languages as long as the person speaking is
sincere and honest! (BTW, throughout this siddur I use Hebrew
words written in Ashkenazic transliteration, and generally did
not translate them in order not to mess up the flow. But there
is an extensive glossary in the back — don’t be shy to use it!)
Of all the “Instant Insights” in this siddur, the following is
my favorite, on the pasuk in Ashrei:
“‫ — קרוב ה׳ לכל קוראיו‬Hashem is close to all who call to Him”;
“‫ — לכל אשר יקראהו באמת‬but one must call out to Hashem with
‫ — אמת‬honesty and sincerity.” It doesn’t say that Hashem is
close to all “‫קראהו באמת‬,” who now call to Him with honesty

XXIII Introduction for Teens

and integrity. Rather, “‫ — אשר יקראהו באמת‬who will call (in the
future tense) to Him with honesty.” This means that if right
now you have a very up-­and-­down relationship with Hashem,
you struggle with being 100 percent sincere 100 percent of
the time, and at times you’re a bit of a hypocrite as far as what
you promise Hashem and what you end up doing — still, as
long as you are committed to being “‫יקראהו באמת‬,” i.e., your
goal is to be completely sincere, honest, and genuine in your
relationship with Hashem, then already now He will listen to
Your requests for help to get there!
If used properly, honestly, and diligently, this siddur will
help you take your tefillah to a higher level, help you get back
up when you’ve had “one of those days,” and give you the tools
to be the best davener you can be. And that is all Hashem
asks of us.

OK — I have a body and a soul.
What can I do to ensure they work together successfully?

I Have a Torah!

‫ברכות התורה‬

‫ָּברו ְּך ַא ָּתה יְ הֹוָ ה ֱאל ֵ ֹֽהינ ּו‬
‫שנ ּו‬
ֽ ָ ׁ ְּ‫ ֲא ׁ ֶשר ִקד‬,‫ֶ ֽמ ֶל ְך ָהעוֹ ָלם‬

all of us, and our children and our
grandchildren, will all acknowledge
Your constant Presence (in our lives)
and we will all study Your Torah for
its own sake (not just to get reward).

5 Shacharis • Hashkamas Ha’boker

I don’t feel, “Oh, my bad luck! I’m stuck with this Torah.” Rather, I’m proud to be
part of this great mission to the world — and therefore I proclaim:
FAQ:
But I do NOT understand the
Torah, and to be honest, I am
NOT happy that I was stuck
being part of the Chosen
People! What’s so great
about it?
ANSWER:
Famous question!
See Appendix 4.
Instant Insight:
As much as we all wish that
our daily experience of Torah
and mitzvos be uplifting,
meaningful, and exciting,
the truth is that for many of
us, at times in our lives it is
not. But the bedrock of our
emunah is that Hashem spoke
at Har Sinai and chose us for a
mission (which is borne out by
our miraculous existence until
today — to the consternation of
much of the rest of the world!),
and therefore, simply put, we
must get with the program!
That is the first brachah of
‫ — לעסוק‬you just have to do it
to the best of your ability. You
have questions? Fine. It will
take a lifetime to ask all the
questions and get answers. But
until that happens, we must
start with ‫ — לעסוק‬get involved
with the Torah, get with the
program, and do the best we
can. As we said earlier: ‫שכל‬
‫ — טוב‬A good understanding of
Torah is given ‫ — לכל עושיהם‬to
those who practice it.
Despite this need to “do it” no
matter what, since we all know
deep down that there must be
“A Plan” and that there is depth
to Torah that we simply don’t
understand yet, we then ask
Hashem ‫— והערב נא‬please, make
it sweet and understandable.
Why? First of all, it is easier to
“take vitamins and medicines”
(it is Torah that keeps us
spiritually healthy) that are
sweet. But in addition, ‫ונהיה אנחנו‬
‫וצאצאינו וצאצאי עמך בית ישראל כלנו‬
‫— יודעי שמך‬it will be easier for us
to hand it down to OUR kids if
we and they enjoy it! We then
end the series of three brachos
by proclaiming that as much as
we do or don’t understand—‫אשר‬
‫— בחר בנו‬we thank Hashem
for choosing us for His great
mission to the world.

,‫ָּברו ְּך ַא ָּתה יְ הֹוָ ה ֱאל ֵֹהינ ּו ֶמ ֶל ְך ָהעוֹ ָלם‬
‫ֲא ֶׁשר ָּב ַחר ָּבנ ּו ִמ ָּכל ָה ַע ִּמים וְ נָ ַתן ָלנ ּו ֶאת‬
:‫ ָּברו ְּך ַא ָּתה יְ הֹוָ ה נוֹ ֵתן ַה ּתוֹ ָרה‬: ֹ‫ּתוֹ ָרתו‬
‫ — ָּברּוְך ַא ָּתה ְיהֹוָה‬You, Hashem, are the Source of
everything; ‫ֹלהינּו‬
ֵ ‫ — ֱא‬You take care of us; ‫עֹולם‬
ָ ‫ — ֶמ ֶלְך ָה‬and
You rule the entire world; ‫( — ֲא ֶׁשר ָּב ַחר ָּבנּו ִמ ָּכל ָה ַע ִּמים‬we
are so happy that) You have chosen us, Bnei Yisrael, out
of all the nations of the world; ‫ּתֹורתֹו‬
ָ ‫ָתן ָלנּו ֶאת‬
ַ ‫ — ְונ‬and
have given us Your Torah. ‫ — ָּברּוְך ַא ָּתה ְיהֹוָה‬You, Hashem,
are the Source of everything; ‫ּתֹורה‬
ָ ‫נֹותן ַה‬
ֵ — Who gives
(us) the Torah.
Now That We Said Birchos HaTorah — We Learn
Some Torah!

‫שחרית • טלית ותפילין‬
And I Am Committed to Putting Torah into Practice

‫ הנחת תפילין‬/ ‫עטיפת טלית‬
‫עטיפת טלית‬
I dedicate my body to serving Hashem, and I’m not afraid to
stand up for the Torah because Hashem will always protect me!

It is important to think a bit before one does a mitzvah
so that one will be doing it for the correct reasons! This is
especially true with the mitzvah of tallis, which is an external
symbol of an inner commitment. Therefore, many say ‫ברכי‬
‫ נפשי‬and ‫ לשם יחוד‬before putting on their tallis.

,‫ָּב ְר ִכי נַ ְפ ׁ ִשי ֶאת יְ הֹוָ ה‬
‫יְ הֹוָ ה ֱאל ַֹהי ָ ּג ַד ְל ָּת ְּמאֹד‬
:‫הוֹ ד וְ ָה ָדר ָל ָב ׁ ְש ָּת‬
‫ע ֶֹטה אוֹ ר ַּכשּׂ ַ ְל ָמה‬
:‫יעה‬
ָ ‫נוֹ ֶטה ׁ ָש ַמיִ ם ַּכיְ ִר‬

‫ַפ ִׁשי‬
ְ ‫ — ָּב ְר ִכי נ‬Just by looking
at Your amazing creation,
my soul calls out that You,
Hashem, are the Source of
it all! And (though) You are
“clothed in majesty and
splendor” and “wrapped
in light” (meaning, Hashem
is beyond our comprehension), You are my personal God and You direct all that happens
here on earth, under the Heavens.
‫ — ְל ֵׁשם יִחּוד‬Just as I am
about to wrap myself in
my tallis, so too may my
neshamah be wrapped
in the light of Hashem.
And may the merit of this
mitzvah protect me from
all spiritual and physical
dangers that I may
encounter during the day.
And (even though I know
that I do not always do all
the mitzvos perfectly and
with the proper intentions)
may Hashem consider this
mitzvah as if I did it 100
percent properly!

‫שחרית • טלית ותפילין‬

8

‫ — ְל ֵׁשם יִחּוד‬I am about to put on tefillin, with the intent to fulfill a mitzvah of the
Torah. What is written in the tefillin reminds me of the great miracles of yetzias
Mitzrayim, and that Hashem controls the world! By putting tefillin on my arm
and opposite my heart, I dedicate my strengths and talents wholeheartedly to the
service of Hashem. By putting tefillin on my head, I dedicate my intellectual powers
and all my senses to the service of Hashem. In the merit of this mitzvah, may I live
a long, productive, and spiritual life — free from improper thoughts. And may the
yetzer hara not sway me to sin — rather it should allow me to serve You — which
is really what my heart desires to do! And (even though I know that I do not always
do all the mitzvos perfectly and with the proper intentions) may Hashem consider this
mitzvah as if I did it 100 percent properly!
Brachah before putting on the tefillin shel yad:

‫ָּברו ְּך ַא ָּתה יְ הֹוָ ה ֱאל ֵֹהינ ּו‬

:‫יח ְּת ִפ ִּלין‬

‫חזֶ ק ָ֔יד הוֹ ִצ ָ ֥‬
‫ִ֚ ּכי ְּב ֣ ֹ‬
‫‪TO SUMMARIZE: I recognize and proclaim that: 1) Hashem woke me up because‬‬
‫‪He thinks I have the ability to fulfill my mission; 2) I also have a working body‬‬
‫‪and soul to use to accomplish that mission; 3) I have a Torah that guides‬‬
‫‪me how to do it right; 4) I am proud to be part of this great mission; 5) I am‬‬
‫‪dedicating myself to put the Torah into practice; and 6) I know Hashem will‬‬
‫!‪protect me while I am trying to accomplish my mission‬‬

Now let’s lay down the basic principles that guide us in that mission (whether
we understand them all as yet — or not!).

‫( — יִגְ ַּדל‬The Thirteen Principles of
Faith) (1) There exists a perfect God
Who created everything. (2) God is
One — there is only one God. (3) God
cannot be viewed in bodily terms.
(4) God is Eternal. (5) Jews pray directly
to God with no intermediaries. (6) The
prophets are true. (7) Moshe was the
greatest prophet. (8) The entire Torah
was given to Moshe. (9) The Torah
is eternal and unchanging. (10) God
knows what we do. (11) God rewards
and punishes according to our actions.
(12) Mashiach will come. (13) There
will be Techiyas Hameisim.

But First — Fifteen Thank-Yous!

‫ברכות השחר‬
FAQ:
Thank you?
For what? My life
stinks!
ANSWER:
Good point! See
Appendix 2

‫ָתן ַל ֶּׂש ְכוִי ִבינָה‬
ַ ‫— ֲא ֶׁשר נ‬
Thank You, for You
give us the ability to
understand the difference between day
and night — meaning:
good and bad, right
and wrong.

‫ָּברו ְּך ַא ָּתה יְ הֹוָ ה ֱאל ֵֹהינ ּו‬
‫ ֲא ׁ ֶשר נָ ַתן‬,‫ֶמ ֶל ְך ָהעוֹ ָלם‬
‫ַלשּׂ ֶ ְכוִ י ִבינָ ה ְל ַה ְב ִחין ֵּבין‬
:‫יוֹ ם ו ֵּבין ָליְ ָלה‬

Instant Insight: Literally, ‫ שכוי‬could be translated as a rooster. The uniqueness of the
rooster is that it starts to crow right before the light of dawn. Night/darkness
represents a lack of clarity and a sense of hopelessness. In the morning light, we
see that all is really not so bad. The rooster teaches us that even in the darkest time
of night, we can see the light if we use the “ability to understand” that Hashem
gave us! We thank Him for that ability.

‫שחרית • ברכות השחר‬

‫ָּברו ְּך ַא ָּתה יְ הֹוָ ה ֱאל ֵֹהינ ּו‬
‫שנִ י‬
ׂ ַ ‫ ׁ ֶשלּ ֹא ָע‬,‫ֶמ ֶל ְך ָהעוֹ ָלם‬
:‫גּ וֹ י‬

‫ — ֶׁשֹּלא ָע ַׂשנִי ּגֹוי‬Thank You,
for You did not create
me a member of another
nation (who have to go
through an entire process
if they want to join the Chosen People. I was given that as a gift at
birth — thank You!).
FAQ:

‫ָּברו ְּך ַא ָּתה יְ הֹוָ ה ֱאל ֵֹהינ ּו‬
‫שנִ י‬
ׂ ַ ‫ ׁ ֶשלּ ֹא ָע‬,‫ֶמ ֶל ְך ָהעוֹ ָלם‬

‫ — ֶׁשֹּלא ָע ַׂשנִי ָע ֶבד‬Thank You,
for You did not create me
a servant (rather, I am free
to make my own choices, be
held responsible for them,
and thus earn reward — thank You!).
For women:

FAQ:
Chosen People?
Doesn’t this brachah
sound racist? I
mean, I know a lot
of really nice nonJews — and some
“not-so-great” Jews!
Is there really such a
difference?
ANSWER:
Common
misconception!
See Appendix 4.

:‫ָע ֶבד‬

For men:

‫שה ִּלי‬
ׂ ָ ‫ ׁ ֶש ָע‬,‫ֶמ ֶל ְך ָהעוֹ ָלם‬
:‫ָּכל ָצ ְר ִּכי‬

‫ — ֶׁש ָע ָׂשה ִּלי ָּכל ָצ ְר ִּכי‬Thank
You, for You have
provided me with all my
needs (whatever You have
given me must be what I
need — not more and not less — even though sometimes that is hard
to understand).

Instant Insight: This brachah is in the singular — specifically about me (gave me all my

needs), unlike so many other brachos. Why? Because I need to feel satisfied with
what I have. But regarding others, my job is not to tell them, “Don’t worry — you
have all you need,” but to go help them!
Instant Insight: This brachah can also be understood in the following manner:
‫ — ֶׁש ָע ָׂשה ִּלי ָּכל ָצ ְר ִּכי‬all the “stuff” in life that You have “given” to me, all the things that
I am going through, as difficult as they may seem, I believe they are all ‫ — ָצ ְר ִּכי‬things
that I need to deal with in order to be the best “me” I can be (even if now I cannot
see how). Thank You!

‫ַוי ְִהי ַא ַחר ַה ְּד ָב ִרים ָה ֵאּלֶה‬

‫‪up during the day.‬‬

‫ישו ָּע ְת ָך‬
ׁ ‫ ו ִּב‬,‫וְ ַק ֵ ּד ׁש ֶאת ִׁש ְמ ָך ָּבעוֹ ָל ֶמ ָך‬
,‫ ָּברו ְּך ַא ָּתה יְ הֹוָ ה‬:ּ‫יה ַק ְרנֵ נו‬

:‫ַה ְמ ַקדֵּ ׁש ֶאת ִׁש ְמך ָּב ַר ִּבים‬
‫להינּו‬
ֵ ‫ַא ָּתה הּוא ְיהֹוָה ֱא‬
Though we will be spending the day taking care of our
personal needs, we declare that we long for the time when
all Jews will return to Eretz Yisrael, the Beis Hamikdash will
be rebuilt, and the world will recognize the truth. And this
leads into the recitation of Korbanos.

FAQ:
Really? Am I
supposed to wake
up each morning
and say to myself:
“I sooo wish I
could go bring an
animal sacrifice
this morning”?
How are we
supposed to relate
to a concept that
seems so foreign
to us?
ANSWER:
Valid question!
See Appendix 9.

:‫ ָא ַמר יְ הֹוָ ה‬,‫יכם‬
ֶ ֵ‫יכם ְל ֵעינ‬
ֶ ‫ׁ ְשבו ֵּת‬
‫קרבנות‬
This section of davening is basically an overview of the daily
avodah in the Beis Hamikdash, and of all korbanos. We say
this to show that we understand that our tefillos are merely
a substitute for the real service of Hashem, which was in the
Beis Hamikdash with korbanos. We express our hope that
we will soon have the opportunity to do that again!

27 Shacharis • Korbanos

‫רבי ישמעאל אומר‬
Because Torah study is so central to our lives — and we might not have a lot of
time for learning during our busy day, we “learn” the preceding Mishnah, and
conclude with “learning” the following Beraisa.
Why conclude with this particular Beraisa?
• One reason is that ‫( ישמעאל‬as in “‫ )”רבי ישמעאל אומר‬means “Hashem will hear.”
As we now begin the core of Shacharis, we need to reinforce in ourselves
that Hashem DOES hear our tefillos, even though there are times in our
lives when it seems to us that He does not.
• According to Kabbalah, the Thirteen Middos of Torah interpretation, as set
down by Rabbi Yishmael, are parallel to the Thirteen Middos Ha’rachamim
(Hashem, Hashem, Kel Rachum … ) of Hashem. By us committing ourselves
to studying Torah and applying it to our lives, Hashem will deal with us
today, and always, based on His middah of Mercy and Forgiveness, rather
than His middah of Strict Judgment.

‫ברייתא דרבי ישמעאל‬

ָ ‫ׂה ָׁשלֹום ִּב ְמ‬
‫עֹוש‬
ֶ — (The person saying Kaddish requests:) May He Who makes peace
in the Heavens bring about peace upon us and upon all of Bnei Yisrael! And to that

29 Shacharis • Pesukei D’Zimrah
Instant Insight:
Before we begin Pesukei
D’Zimrah with its opening
brachah of Baruch
She’amar, we say this
chapter of Tehillim that
David HaMelech created
for the eventual Chanukas
HaBayis — inauguration of
the Beis Hamikdash — which
interestingly enough, he
was not allowed to build!
How is this relevant to us
today, and what does it have
to do with Pesukei D’Zimrah
and the Beis Hamikdash?
This chapter discusses
the difficulties (physical,
emotional, and spiritual)
that David HaMelech faced
in his lifetime, and his belief
that Hashem could and
would pull him through it
all. He also expresses his
belief that eventually his
labors would bear fruit
(the Beis Hamikdash would
be built), even though he
knew it was ordained by
Hashem that he would not
live to see it! Yet he did
the best he could with the
struggles Hashem sent his
way and thanked Hashem
for all he did accomplish.
We too have no idea
whether Hashem will allow
us to see the end results
of all our hard work! Yet
we will now say Pesukei
D’Zimrah and thank
Hashem for all He has done
and proclaim our belief
that He controls all the
circumstances in our lives.
We will say the Shema and
proclaim our loyalty to His
Torah through thick and
thin, and based on that, we
will ask Him to give us the
things we think we need
in order to accomplish our
mission (Shemoneh Esrei).
And we do this with joy
and faith even though we
do not know whether we
will merit to see it all to
fruition or not!
Importantly, the Malbim
says that this chapter really
refers to our body — also
a mini Beis Hamikdash — a
physical thing that we fill
with kedushah and use to
fulfill our mission. And, like
the real Beis Hamikdash,
it must be treated with
respect and sanctity!

‫פסוקי דזמרה‬
Some find Pesukei D’Zimrah “annoying”—all this
praising of Hashem strikes them as strange. “What
does He need that for?” Of course, Hashem doesn’t
need our praises—we need to praise Him! For an
explanation of this crucial idea, see the Introduction
to Birkas Ha’mazon on page 119.
Much of Pesukei D’Zimrah is made up of Tehillim, which is poetry. The
nuances and beauty of the various paragraphs are too easily lost,
which is why some people have the unfortunate “custom” of saying
just Baruch She’amar, Ashrei, and Yishtabach. Here, we will try to
convey the point of each part, but this can never do justice to the
depth, beauty, and lofty spiritual ideas David HaMelech wove into
every sentence. Still, any improvement counts!

Introduction to Pesukei D’Zimrah

(for we believe that they are all fair and just). ‫ — ָּברּוְך ַא ָּתה ְיהֹוָה‬You,
Hashem, are the Source of everything; ‫ֻּלל ַּב ִּת ְׁש ָּבחֹות‬
ָ ‫ — ֶמ ֶלְך ְמה‬the
King Who is praised by all (including me!).

‫הודו ליהוה קראו בשמו‬
Since Hodu is very long and a collection of various pesukim and statements
(rather than being one chapter of Tehillim), we are going to break it up into
sections and focus on a few key phrases.

‫ — הֹודּו‬Even though I am now
going to personally praise
Hashem for all He does, and
focus on His power in nature
and history (which will then
lead me to personally accept
His sovereignty on myself — the
Shema), really the entire world
should acknowledge the power
of Hashem and praise Him.
Hashem chose our Avos and
Imahos, turned them into a
great nation and miraculously
gave us Eretz Yisrael (and we
are the center of world attention).
No power throughout history
has been able to destroy us.
(Our very centrality on the
world stage is itself the greatest
proof of Hashem’s power and
our special bond with Him.) For
that we praise Hashem, and
it provides us with a sense of
security and joy.
‫ ַּב ְּׂשרּו ִמּיֹום ֶאל‬,‫ִׁשירּו ַליהֹוָה ָּכל ָה ָא ֶרץ‬
‫ְׁשּועתֹו‬
ָ ‫ — יֹום י‬We await the day
(when Mashiach comes) and the
entire world will acknowledge
the truth, and see the power
of Hashem in history and in
the beauty and majesty of
nature. And the entire Creation
will proclaim:

Serve Hashem with Simchah

34

Instant Insight:
Why do we say
Mizmor L’Sodah daily
when the original
Korban Todah was
brought only when a
person miraculously
survived some
life-threatening
situation (like when
we “bentch gomel”
today)? In truth,
every minute of our
day is a miracle — we
just don’t realize
it! To demand that
every individual go to
the Beis Hamikdash
every day and offer
a Korban Todah is
too great a burden.
But now that tefillah
has taken the place
of korbanos, we say
Mizmor L’Sodah daily
to make us appreciate
the “natural” miracles
we take for granted.
Instant Insight:
Since this is really
about the Korban
Todah, why is it
not in the section
of Korbanos? The
answer is that this
korban is very
different. The Midrash
tells us that when
Mashiach comes,
the only personal
voluntary korban
that will be brought
will be the Korban
Todah. Why? Because
even when Mashiach
comes, it will still be
a struggle for us not
to take for granted all
the good we have!
Instant Insight:
It is so hard to
“serve Hashem with
happiness and peace
of mind (‫”)בשמחה‬
when our lives seem
so difficult! The
key — the word ‫בשמחה‬
rearranged spells the
word ‫ — מחשבה‬thinking.
Happiness and
peace of mind does
not happen by
itself. It comes from
choosing to look
at your life from a
certain perspective.
If you put your mind
(‫ )מחשבה‬to it — you can
have peace of mind
(‫!)בשמחה‬

Hashem as Master of Nature, Hashem as Master of History

FAQ:

Answer:
Complex and deep

Instant Insight:
We are referred
to as the “‫עם‬
‫ — סגולה‬special
treasure” of

word segulah is
related to the
word “segol” — the
three-dot triangle
nekudah that
appears under
letters (that makes

matter which way
you turn the segol,
it remains the very
same shape — the
same segol.
Similarly, Bnei
Yisrael, no matter
where we are or
what choices we
might make, we are
always the unique
nation of Hashem.
We cannot escape

36

Last Six Chapters of Tehillim — the Core of Pesukei D’Zimrah

‫ הללו אל‬/‫ שירו ליהוה‬/‫ הללו את יהוה‬/‫ כי טוב‬/‫ הללי נפשי‬:‫ הללויה‬/ ‫אשרי‬
‫אשרי‬
Ashrei is the core of Pesukei D’Zimrah. If one has limited time, one should at
least say Ashrei. It is said three times a day (twice in Shacharis and once at
Minchah). It follows the aleph-beis. The significance is that in all areas of our
life (aleph to tav) — personally and nationally — we need Hashem and His help,
we are supported by Him, and His providence is constant. And this is true not
only when our lives seem clear and bright (Shacharis — the morning), but also
when things seem to be getting dark and not so bright or clear (Minchah — as
the sun sets). Because of its importance, it is explained here line by line. The
remaining five chapters of Tehillim will be explained with a basic overview of
each chapter.
For a set of different Instant Insights on Ashrei, please see the Ashrei of Minchah.

‫תהילים קמה‬
The following is a preface to Tehillim 145, which begins below. These words
express that it is a wonderful and joyous opportunity to praise Hashem and
not, chas v’shalom, a burden!

‫ עוֹ ד‬,‫ית ָך‬
ֶ ‫ַא ׁ ְש ֵרי יוֹ ׁ ְש ֵבי ֵב‬
‫ ַא ׁ ְש ֵרי ָה ָעם‬:‫יְ ַה ְללו ָּך ּ ֶס ָלה‬
‫ ַא ׁ ְש ֵרי ָה ָעם ׁ ֶשיְ הֹוָ ה‬, ֹ‫ׁ ֶש ָּכ ָכה לו‬
:‫ֱאל ָֹהיו‬

‫יתָך‬
ֶ ‫יֹוׁש ֵבי ֵב‬
ְ ‫ — ַא ְׁש ֵרי‬Happy are those
who (wherever they are) are living
in the house of Hashem (living a

ַ ‫ — עֹוד י‬they will

as opportunities for growth).‫ַא ְׁש ֵרי‬
‫ — ָה ָעם ֶׁש ָּכ ָכה לֹו‬Happy and fortunate are we, the nation (Bnei Yisrael) that has all
this (glorious history and great mission); ‫ֹלהיו‬
ָ ‫— ַא ְׁש ֵרי ָה ָעם ֶׁש ְיהֹוָה ֱא‬happy and fortunate
are we, the nation (Bnei Yisrael) that has Hashem as our personal God Who takes
care of us.

‫ ֲארוֹ ִמ ְמ ָך ֱאלוֹ ַהי‬,‫ְּת ִה ָּלה ְל ָדוִ ד‬

Instant Insight:
There is no ‫ נ‬in
Ashrei — why? The
Maharal says that
these pesukim,
starting with the
letters of the
aleph-beis, express
the inherent
meaning (based on
Kabbalah) of those
letters. The inherent
meaning of ‫ נ׳‬is
‫ — נפילה‬a falling. Here
David HaMelech
is describing what
Hashem does, and
Hashem does not
cause our fallings!
Our mistakes are
the result of our
own poor choices!
In the next verse
we are told what
Hashem does
in regard to our
fallings — ‫סֹומְך ְיהֹוָה ְל ָכל‬
ֵ
‫ — ַהּנ ְֹפ ִלים‬He helps us
get back up!

Insight:
It does not say

‫תהילים קמו‬
The focus of this chapter is on the individual — each person praises Hashem in
their own way and through their own life; and Hashem’s providence is on each
individual in the world.

‫תהילים קמז‬
The focus of this chapter is on Am Yisrael as a whole, and
on the dawn of Era of Mashiach — when the power and
providence of Hashem in our individual and national lives
will finally begin to become clear.
Instant Insight:
‫ְרּוׁש ִַלם‬
ָ ‫ ּבֹונֵה י‬literally translates
as, “Hashem is
building Yerushalayim” — present
tense — even though
in context it is
referring to the Era
of Mashiach. That
is telling us that
in truth, every
single thing that
happened and
happens to us as
individuals and as a
nation is part of the
process needed to
build Yerushalayim.
Thus all through
our galus, all that
Hashem has sent
our way is part of a
continuous building
of Yerushalayim.
Once Mashiach
comes, that will all
be clear.

‫תהילים קמט‬
The focus of this chapter is on the next stage — the new era when the entire
world will join with Bnei Yisrael to sing praises to Hashem — and submit to
His Will.

‫ ִׁשירּו ַליהֹוָה ִׁשיר ָח ָדׁש‬,‫ — ַה ְללּויָּה‬All
will sing a new song of praise to
Hashem; ‫ידים‬
ִ ‫ — ְּת ִה ָּלתֹו ִּב ְק ַהל ֲח ִס‬He
will be praised by all righteous
peoples. (But even in that new era)
we, Bnei Yisrael, will be especially
joyous, since Hashem had always
been our King, and made us
His unique nation (long before
Mashiach’s arrival). And Hashem
has special favor for us since we
showed our allegiance to His Torah
(even when life was difficult and
not clear)!
‫ — ַל ֲעׂשֹות ָּב ֶהם ִמ ְׁש ָּפט ָּכתּוב‬And in that
era, Hashem will mete out the final
punishments to all those nations
who did evil, as is clearly foretold in
the Torah; ‫ידיו‬
ָ ‫ — ָה ָדר הּוא ְל ָכל ֲח ִס‬and
bring everlasting glory to His
righteous ones. (And therefore );
‫ — ַה ְללּויָּה‬whatever happens, praise
Hashem!

‫תהילים קנ‬
This chapter, the very last chapter of the book of Tehillim, sums up the
previous chapters.

‫ ַה ְללּו ֵאל ְּב ָק ְדׁשֹו‬,‫ — ַה ְללּויָּה‬We proclaim
the greatness of Hashem as seen by
His acts that are above nature, and
as seen by His providence within
nature. We praise Hashem with
every sort of musical instrument
(meaning — with all our sometimesconflicting thoughts, emotions, and
no matter what mood we are in — at
all times and in all circumstances— we
sing out to Hashem, knowing that all
is for our best).
‫יָּה‬
‫ְּת ַהּלֵל‬
‫ְׁש ָמה‬
ָ ‫ַהּנ‬
‫ — ּכֹל‬Every
neshamah — every
individual —
should praise Hashem; our entire
complex neshamah should, as one
harmonious whole, praise Hashem;
every breath we take (all that we
do) should bring praise to Hashem.
(And therefore ); ‫ — ַה ְללּויָּה‬whatever
happens, praise Hashem!

, ֹ‫ ַה ְלל ּו ֵאל ְּב ָק ְד ׁשו‬,‫ַה ְללוּיָ ּה‬
‫ ַה ְללוּה ּו‬: ֹ‫יע ֻעזּו‬
ַ ‫ַה ְללוּה ּו ִּב ְר ִק‬
‫ְּכרֹב‬
‫ַה ְללוּה ּו‬
,‫ִבגְ ב ּור ָֹתיו‬
,‫ ַה ְללוּה ּו ְּב ֵת ַקע ׁשוֹ ָפר‬: ֹ‫ֻ ּג ְדלו‬
‫ ַה ְללוּה ּו‬:‫ַה ְללוּה ּו ְּבנֵ ֶבל וְ ִכנּוֹ ר‬
‫ ַה ְללוּה ּו ְּב ִמ ִ ּנים‬,‫ְבתֹף ו ָּמחוֹ ל‬
,‫ ַה ְללוּה ּו ְב ִצ ְל ְצ ֵלי ׁ ָש ַמע‬:‫וְ עוּגָ ב‬
‫ ּכֹל‬:‫ַה ְללוּה ּו ְּב ִצ ְל ְצ ֵלי ְתרו ָּעה‬
:‫ ַה ְללוּיָ ּה‬,‫ַה ְ ּנ ׁ ָש ָמה ְּת ַה ֵּלל יָ ּה‬
,‫ּכֹל ַה ְ ּנ ׁ ָש ָמה ְּת ַה ֵּלל יָ ּה‬
:‫ַה ְללוּיָ ּה‬

And to Sum Up Pesukei D’Zimrah

‫ברוך יהוה לעולם‬

:‫ָּברו ְּך יְ הֹוָ ה ְלעוֹ ָלם ָא ֵמן וְ ָא ֵמן‬

45 Shacharis • Pesukei D’Zimrah

Introduction to Shiras Ha’yam

‫ וכרות עמו הברית‬/ ‫ויברך דוד‬
Before we start asking Hashem for the stuff (we think) we need
(in Shemoneh Esrei), we say Pesukei D’Zimrah to thank Him
for what we do have, and to emphasize our awareness and
belief that it is Hashem alone Who controls our circumstances.
But why do we want all this stuff in the first place? The answer
should be, because we want all the tools possible to fulfill our
part of this great mission to the world called Am Yisrael!
FAQ:
Well, to be honest,
I just want all this
stuff because — I
want it! Is that so
bad?
ANSWER:
Honest question!
See Appendix 8.

Therefore, the climax of Pesukei D’Zimrah will be the Shiras
Ha’yam — the praise Bnei Yisrael sang when Hashem clearly
showed us and the world that we are His special nation with
a special mission. This Shiras Ha’yam is introduced with
the following two paragraphs. The first (‫ְב ֶרְך ָּדוִד‬
ָ ‫ ) ַוי‬is David
Hamelech speaking to the entire nation at the end of his life.
He handed over to his son Shlomo all that he had collected
for the building of the Beis Hamikdash (which he knew he
would not live to see) and looks back at all he went through
and expresses his hopes for the future. The second (‫)א ָּתה הּוא‬
ַ
was said by the Navi Nechemiah when that Beis Hamikdash
lay in ruins! Now Bnei Yisrael are being urged to come
back and make the sacrifices necessary to rebuild the Beis
Hamikdash in particular, and the overall glory of our mission.
The message: Whether we feel we have worked hard,
accomplished much, and have great hopes for the future, or
we feel that all has come crashing down and we are looking
for the energy to climb back up — we simply praise Hashem
for what we do have. Most importantly, we focus on the
fact that we just need to do the best we can, and the final
outcome is in the Hands of Hashem.
Stand until after Barchu

of the opening
four words — ‫ויברך‬
’‫ —דוד את ה‬spell
“‫ודאי‬,” one of the
Names of Hashem,
which represents
His attribute
of certainty
and reliability.
Notwithstanding
all the difficulties
in his life, David
HaMelech always
lived with the belief
that Hashem can
be relied on to take
care of him.

‫ָׁשיר מ ֶֹׁשה‬
ִ ‫ — ָאז י‬Then Moshe and Bnei
Yisrael chose to sing this Shirah
to Hashem and said: I will sing to
Hashem for He has triumphed over
the haughty (Egyptians); His strength
and power saved me. This is my God (I
will create my own personal relationship
with Hashem); and He is the God of
my father (and that unique relationship
will be built on the foundations of our
ancestors). Hashem cast Pharaoh’s
chariots and army into the sea and
they all drowned; at the same time
the flowing waters stood like walls (to
save us). The enemy said, “I will chase
them, overtake them, and obliterate
them!” You made the wind blow, and
the sea covered them, and they sank
like lead!
‫ — ִמי ָכמ ָֹכה ָּב ֵא ִלם ְיהֹוָה‬Among all the
powers (powers of nature, powers
of humans), who is equal to You,
Hashem?; ‫ֶא ָּדר ַּבּקֹדֶׁש‬
ְ ‫ — ִמי ָּכמ ָֹכה נ‬Who is
like You, powerful but with purity of
purpose?; ‫נֹורא ְת ִהֹּלת ע ֵֹׂשה ֶפ ֶלא‬
ָ — Our
songs praising You instill in us awe
of Your wondrous deeds, which are in
truth beyond our comprehension. All
the nations of the world shuddered
with fear (when they heard of this great
miracle). May dread and fear fall upon
them until Your nation crosses over
(into Eretz Yisrael), the place Your
Presence is most felt.

Concluding Brachah of Pesukei D’Zimrah

‫ישתבח‬
We do not fully realize all the good You do for us; nor do we have the ability to
adequately thank and praise You. Thus we end Pesukei D’Zimrah by saying simply:

,ּ‫יִ ׁ ְש ַּת ַּבח ׁ ִש ְמ ָך ָל ַעד ַמ ְל ֵּכנו‬

,‫ נֶ ַצח‬,‫ עֹז ו ֶּמ ְמ ׁ ָש ָלה‬,‫וְ זִ ְמ ָרה‬

‫שידק‬/‫שחרית • וכרב‬

‫ברכו‬/‫קדיש‬
In Pesukei D’Zimrah we thanked Hashem for all He does for the universe and for
us specifically, and we focused on the truth that Hashem is the only Source for
everything. In the section after Barchu—the Shema—we will accept and affirm
our responsibility to use all He has given us toward the mission He put us here
for! Before we do that, we “break” with Kaddish and Barchu, which sum up that
mission! And what is that mission? That the entire world should recognize that
Hashem is the Source of all and that all will “bless His Name,” i.e., will realize that
all He does (in general, and for each individual) and all that He commands is
perfect and for the ultimate best.

‫חצי קדיש‬
‫ּדל ְוי ְִת ַק ַּדׁש ְׁש ֵמּה ַר ָּבא‬

:‫ ָּב ְרכ ּו ֶאת יְ הֹוָ ה ַה ְּמב ָֹר ְך‬:‫חזן‬

We must make the world realize (by how we speak and how we
conduct ourselves daily) that Hashem is the Source of all, and that
everything He does is perfect and for the ultimate best.

meaning into the
material world
and to allow
the Presence of
Hashem to be
recognized by
all (through us
leading our daily
lives guided by His
mitzvos). However,
as individuals,
we are limited in
scope and power.
It is only when
we are a tzibbur
working together
as a multicolored,
harmonious
unit that we can
truly accomplish
our goal. Thus
this public
proclamation of
our life’s goal must
be made only as a
tzibbur.

‫ָעד‬
ֶ ‫עֹולם ו‬
ָ ‫( — ָּברּוְך ְיהֹוָה ַה ְּמב ָֹרְך ְל‬And we respond, followed by the chazzan himself:) Yes, we
pledge to make the world realize (by how we speak and how we conduct ourselves daily)
that Hashem is the Source of all, and that everything He does is perfect and for the
ultimate best — for all eternity!
Our Mission Statement!

‫קריאת שמע וברכותיה‬
Chazal structured this section by having us say two brachos before we say the
Shema (the first is a very long one made up of different parts, and the second
is one paragraph) and one brachah after we say the Shema. Then we will be
ready for our one-on-one with Hashem — the Shemoneh Esrei.
We Are Responsible for Sanctifying the World through Our Actions

‫ — ֶאת ֵׁשם ָה ֵאל ַה ֶּמ ֶלְך‬And all angels accept their individualized job, and work together
in harmony to carry out the will of their Creator (a message to us: each of us has
different strengths and unique talents, and to do our mission we must be united and work
with each other!). And in unison they all proclaim:

‫ ְמלֹא ָכל‬,‫ָקדוֹ ׁש ָקדוֹ ׁש ָקדוֹ ׁש יְ הֹוָ ה ְצ ָבאוֹ ת‬
: ֹ‫ָה ָא ֶרץ ְּכבוֹ דו‬
1. Hashem is ‫ — קדוש‬He is separate and beyond the comprehension of all creations,
from we humans and up to the highest angels; ‫ — ְמֹלא ָכל ָה ָא ֶרץ ְּכבֹודֹו‬His Presence
is everywhere.
2. Hashem is ‫ — קדוש‬The sanctity of Hashem can be felt in all worlds, from the very
highest world of angels down to the very lowest world, our material world; ‫ְמֹלא‬
‫ — ָכל ָה ָא ֶרץ ְּכבֹודֹו‬His Presence can be felt everywhere (though it requires effort and
diligence in order to tap into it through Torah and mitzvos.)

Those two understandings of ‫ קדוש‬seem like a contradiction, but:

‫ש ִאים ְל ֻע ַּמת‬
ׂ ְ ּ ַ‫וְ ָהאוֹ ַפ ִ ּנים וְ ַח ּיוֹ ת ַהק ֶֹד ׁש ְּב ַר ַע ׁש ָ ּגדוֹ ל ִמ ְתנ‬
:‫ ְל ֻע ָּמ ָתם ְמ ׁ ַש ְּב ִחים וְ אוֹ ְמ ִרים‬,‫ש ָר ִפים‬
ְׂ
‫אֹופּנִים‬
ַ ‫ְה‬
ָ ‫ — ו‬The Ofanim and the holy Chayos, together with the Serafim (all the highest
levels of angels — part of the spiritual reality that is beyond our material understanding)
all proclaim:

: ֹ‫ָּברו ְּך ְּכבוֹ ד יְ הֹוָ ה ִמ ְּמקוֹ מו‬

‫ — ָּברּוְך ְּכבֹוד ְיהֹוָה‬The essence of Hashem is completely beyond us (even the highest
angels); it is ‫ — ִמ ְּמקֹומֹו‬from His place (up there, it all makes sense!).
Thus we accept that Hashem is beyond our comprehension, and at the same time,
we know that His Presence can be felt everywhere if we invest the time and effort
to look for it. Therefore, we continue:

‫ִּתנּו‬
ֵ ‫ — ְל ֵאל ָּברּוְך נְ ִעימֹות י‬All praises
go to Hashem, for He alone is the
Creator of inventions (He gives
us the intelligence and inspiration
to discover the powers He placed in
the universe); He controls all wars;
keeps an account of our good deeds
so they may be used to bring about
future salvation for us; controls
our health; and renews Creation
continually (thus our obligation to
use it all according to His laws).

ִ ׁ ‫שה ְב ֵר‬

:‫ַה ְּמאוֹ רוֹ ת‬

Instant Insight: ‫ח ָדׁש‬
ָ ‫ — אֹור‬literally means “a new light.” We refer to the arrival of

Mashiach in terms of “light” to stress that the main reason we await Mashiach is
not to simply solve our material problems and discomforts! Rather, we want all of
Bnei Yisrael (and the whole world) to see the light, beauty, and truth of Torah. (‫אור‬
has the gematriya of 207, which adds up to 9; ‫ אמת‬is 441, which adds up to 9.)

54

We Are Obligated to Learn Torah and to Apply the Letter and Spirit of the Law

fact that we may not always have

55 Shacharis • Birchos Krias Shema

Important Instant
Insight:
The idea of an
“‫ — עול‬yoke,” as in the
concept of “‫עול מלכות‬
‫ — שמים‬accepting the
yoke of the Kingdom of
Heaven,” seems to carry
an image of mitzvos
being a heavy, difficult
burden that we shlep
around on our backs our
whole life. True, at times
mitzvos seem “heavy”
and difficult, and we are
obligated to persevere.
But the choice of
the term ‫ עול‬carries a
profound message.
As I heard from Rav
Matisyahu Solomon,
shlita, a yoke is simply
a tool that allows the
animal to do its job in
the most efficient and
easy way possible. How
else could it pull the
wagon if it did not have
a yoke? So too, when
we proclaim the Shema
and publicly accept ‫עול‬
‫מלכות שמים‬, the yoke of
the Kingdom of Heaven,
we must realize that the
mitzvos are perfectly
crafted, precise tools
that allow us to do
OUR job here on earth!
How would we manage
without them?
Instant Insight:
The letters of the word
‫ע‬-‫מ‬-‫ ש‬are the same as
the letters that begin
the words ‫עול מלכות שמים‬
but backward! The
message is that even
when Torah laws and
values seem “backward”
to us — as if they make
no sense — nevertheless
we accept that in truth
they are the tools for
our success.
Instant Insight:
In the Torah, the
pasuk of the Shema
has the letters ‫ע׳‬
and ‫( ד׳‬which spell
‫ — עד‬witness) enlarged.
The very miracle of our
survival — that after all
these years Jews all over
the world are united in
their proclamation of
the Shema — is witness
to the truth of the Torah!

OK, I’ll Do It!
‫קריאת שמע — עול מלכות שמים‬

‫ ויאמר‬/ ‫ והיה‬/ ‫ ואהבת‬/ ‫ ברוך שם‬/ ‫שמע‬
There are many levels of meaning to this crucial pasuk
that we should have in mind as we proclaim the essence
of our belief. Here we will present two of the most basic
concepts one should have in mind when reciting the
Shema.
If you are davening without a minyan, add the following three words:

‫ֶא ָמן‬
ֱ ‫ֵאל ֶמ ֶלְך נ‬
Hashem is a merciful, fair, and trustworthy King.

‫ש ָר ֵא֑ל יְ הֹוָ ֥ה ֱאל ֵֹה֖ינ ּו יְ הֹוָ ֥ה ׀ ֶא ָ ֽחד׃‬
ׂ ְ ִ‫ׁ ְש ַ ֖מע י‬
:‫ ָּברו ְּך ּׁ ֵשם ְּכבוֹ ד ַמ ְלכוּתוֹ ְלעוֹ ָלם וָ ֶעד‬:‫בלחש‬
‫ִׂש ָר ֵאל‬
ְ ‫ — ְׁש ַמע י‬As a proud member of Bnei Yisrael, I publicly
proclaim; that ‫ — ְיהֹוָה‬the Eternal God, Who always was, is,
and will be; ‫ֹלהינּו‬
ֵ ‫ — ֱא‬I accept as our personal, all-powerful God
(with all the responsibilities that come with that!); ‫ — ְיהֹוָה‬and
that God, Who controls all aspects of my life and of the
universe; is ‫ — ֶא ָחד‬the One and Only controlling power.
In addition, one should have in mind:

‫ִׂש ָר ֵאל‬
ְ ‫— ְׁש ַמע י‬As a proud member of Bnei Yisrael, I publicly
proclaim; that ‫— ְיהֹוָה‬the Name that represents God’s attribute
of mercy; ‫ֹלהינּו‬
ֵ ‫— ֱא‬is the same God as Elokim, the Name that
represents His attribute of strict judgment; ‫— ְיהֹוָה ֶא ָחד‬it is all
the same God; all is merciful, since all that He does is for our
good (just that sometimes the “medicine” is bitter).
Quietly:

‫ — ָּברּוְך ֵׁשם ְּכבֹוד ַמ ְלכּותֹו‬May the perception of Hashem’s
Presence and rulership be increased (so all humans will
recognize His existence and power);
‫ָעד‬
ֶ ‫עֹולם ו‬
ָ ‫ — ְל‬forever and ever.

‫ — ִמי ָכמ ָֹכה ָּב ֵא ִלים ְיהֹוָה‬Among all the powers (powers of nature, powers of humans), who

And now that we are firm in our conviction that Hashem has
chosen us, has taken care of us, will take care of us, and is
the only power Who controls all elements of our lives, we
come before Him with our requests— so that we will have all
we need in order to do our unique part in this great mission
in the best way possible.

OUR REQUESTS

‫שמנה עשרה‬

bad?

question! See
Appendix 8.

Opening Statement

:‫ש ָפ ַתי ִּת ְפ ָּתח ו ִּפי יַ ִ ּגיד ְּת ִה ָּל ֶת ָך‬
ׂ ְ ,‫ֲאדֹנָ י‬
‫ּופי י ִַּגיד ְּת ִה ָּל ֶתָך‬
ִ ‫ׂפ ַתי ִּת ְפ ָּתח‬
ָ ‫ ְש‬,‫( — ֲאדֹנָי‬I am standing here because I do
want to daven, create a relationship, and thank You for all the good
You do for me — which is sometimes hard to see or understand — and I
want to ask You for the help I need. But that does not come easy to me
for so many reasons. Therefore, I am asking, please) Hashem, open
my lips and help me express myself properly.

]Instant Insight:
The terms ‫עוזר‬, ‫מושיע‬,
and ‫ מגן‬represent
three levels of help
that we need in
our lives — from the
least to the most.
‫“ — עוזר‬help,” is
when we are on the
right track, doing
what we need to
do in the best way
possible, and we
ask Hashem to
please help us that
all should work out,
since nothing will
come to fruition
without His help.
‫“ — מושיע‬salvation,”
is when we are
completely at a
loss; we throw up
our hands and
say to Hashem,
“Save me, I have
no idea what to
do next!” The third
and highest level,
‫“ — מגן‬protect,” is
when we think
we’re on the right
track and think we
know what we’re
doing, but in truth
we are completely
off base! We ask
Hashem, “If I’m
going in the wrong
direction — ‫— מגן‬
protect me from
myself!”

‫קדושה‬
Let us praise Hashem like the angels do!

Unlike angels who have a higher understanding of Hashem, we humans cannot
understand “Who” Hashem is — His very essence (represented by the word
“‫)”אותו‬. All we can perceive is what He does — referred to as “‫שמך‬.” Nevertheless,
in Kedushah we will praise Hashem like the angels do. Since we can only do
this when we are at the level of a tzibbur — a unified community — we therefore
only say this with a minyan.

city of Yerushalayim; and let Your

promised; and rebuild it (the city
and the Beis Hamikdash) soon,
in our days, as an everlasting

of David HaMelech. ‫ָּברּוְך ַא ָּתה‬

ָׁ ‫ — ּבֹונֵה י‬Who builds Yerushalayim (present tense — since every
step of our galus is a necessary part of the process of the eventual permanent rebuilding
of Yerushalayim).

Instant Insight:
Why do we say,
“Listen to our voice
(‫ ?”)קולנו‬Shouldn’t
we ask Hashem to
listen to the words
we say? What we
are asking Hashem
is: “I may not be
so articulate, and
I may not know
how to say what
I really feel. But I
hope You can tell
from the sound
of my voice that
I am doing the
best I can to
communicate with
You!”
Crucial Instant
Insight:
We asked Hashem
to “accept our
tefillos,” so what
are we adding by
requesting, “Do
not turn us away
empty-handed”?
Of course, we
hope that Hashem
will grant us
what we have
requested. But
sometimes, for
reasons known
only to Him,
Hashem must say
no. We therefore
ask Hashem that
if the answer to
our tefillah must
be a no, then at
least He should
not send us away
feeling “emptyhanded” — with
a feeling of total
rejection. Let our
hearts be filled
with emunah
that we had the
amazing zechus
and opportunity
to talk one-on-one
with Hashem and
that He heard our
tefillah, even if the
best thing for us
right now is a no.

(Now stop for a second. Here is your big chance! This is where you
may add any and all personal requests you have! Whatever is in your
heart and on your mind, don’t be shy to speak it out now. Hashem
understands all languages and He listens!)

On Rosh Chodesh and Chol Hamoed add Yaaleh V’Yavo: (If you forgot, see Appendix 12:6)

‫בֹותינּו‬
ֵ ‫ֵאֹלהי ֲא‬
ֵ ‫ֹלהינּו ו‬

‫ ֵמעוֹ ָלם‬,‫יך‬
:‫ִקוִ ּינ ּו ָל ְך‬

Instant Insight:
By all other brachos
of the Shemoneh
Esrei, we simply
respond “Amen”
to the chazzan’s
brachah. Why
here must we say
our own “Modim,”
as if an Amen is
not sufficient? All
other brachos are
requests. When you
are with someone
else and they
put in a request
for something
important, saying
simply, “And
me too — I also
agree!” is fine. He
represents you.
That is Amen.
But if someone is
really bursting with
gratitude, especially
to Hashem, for
“our lives, which
are entrusted into
Your Hand” and
“for our miraculous
survival till
today,” then Amen
is impossible. No
one can “represent”
your inner feeling
of gratitude! It
must burst forth!
Thus, Modim
D’Rabbanan!

are entrusted into Your
Hand, and our souls,
which are in Your care; ‫ְעל‬
ַ‫ו‬
‫ִסיָך ֶׁש ְּב ָכל יֹום ִע ָּמנּו‬
ֶּ ‫ — נ‬and for
Your (hidden) miracles that
are with us every day (and
we do not even realize it!);
and for Your wonders and
Your acts of goodness that
occur at all times, evening,
morning, and afternoon (literally, twenty-four hours a day, as well
as figuratively, during the dark times in our lives, the clear bright
times, and the hazy twilight times). ‫ ִּכי ֹלא ָכלּו ַר ֲח ֶמיָך‬,‫ — ַהּטֹוב‬You
are the Benevolent One, for Your compassion is never-ending;
‫ ִּכי ֹלא ַתּמּו ֲח ָסדֶיָך‬,‫ְה ְּמ ַר ֵחם‬
ַ ‫ — ו‬You are the Merciful One, for Your
loving-kindness is never exhausted. ‫עֹולם ִק ִוּינּו ָלְך‬
ָ ‫ — ֵמ‬From the
beginning of time, we have placed our hope in You.

Birkas Kohanim is said by the chazzan (and in some places in Israel by Kohanim)
during the repetition of the Shemoneh Esrei:
Instant Insight:
Generally, we think
of “‫ — שלום‬peace”
as meaning people
getting along with
each other. But if
so, then the third
brachah should
have said “‫וישם לכם‬
‫ — שלום‬Hashem will
give peace to you,”
plural, i.e., to the
group. Why is the
word “‫ — לך‬to you,”
singular, used? The
singular is used
because you cannot
get along with
others if you first
do not get along
with yourself! Thus,
this brachah is to
the individual — that
each person should
have inner serenity
and peace. Only
then can we live in
peace with others.

‫בֹותינּו‬
ֵ ‫ֵאֹלהי ֲא‬
ֵ ‫ֹלהינּו ו‬

everything

‫ש ָר ֵאל‬
ׂ ְ ִ‫יך ְל ָב ֵר ְך ֶאת ַע ְּמ ָך י‬
:‫ְּב ָכל ֵעת ו ְּב ָכל ׁ ָש ָעה ִּב ׁ ְשלוֹ ֶמ ָך‬

(Peace of mind, inner peace:) ‫ׂים ָׁשלֹום‬
‫ִש‬
‫ּוב ָר ָכה‬
ְ ‫טֹובה‬
ָ —Hashem, grant each
of us an inner sense of well-being
(by showering us with) goodness,
blessings, favor, loving-kindness,
and compassion (through which we
can easily recognize the love You have
for us; thus our soul will feel at peace
with You). And may this serenity
extend to all members of Bnei
Yisrael, Your nation.
(Peace between one another:) ‫ָּב ְרכֵנּו‬
‫ ֻּכ ָּלנּו ְּכ ֶא ָחד‬,‫ — ָא ִבינּו‬Our Father , bless
all of us together to become one
unified nation, by allowing us to
all equally see Your Presence (and thanks to such clarity, discord will cease!). For You,
Hashem, when we were in Your direct Divine Presence (at Har Sinai), gave us a living
Torah (which teaches us how to live together in harmony), and You also gave us a love of
doing kindness and righteousness. (This all leads to everyone sharing) their blessings
with each other, living with compassion, and thus having a full life and living in peace
(by forgiving others).
(Peace from war and danger:) ‫ — וְטֹוב ְּב ֵעינֶיָך ְל ָב ֵרְך ֶאת ַע ְּמָך‬And may it be favorable in Your
eyes to bless Your people, Bnei Yisrael, at all times and at every moment, with Your
peace (protection from wars and dangers).

Your thirteen attributes of mercy (‫י״ג‬
‫)מדות הרחמים‬, and You will listen to

says in the Torah:

The following is only said with a minyan:

:‫וַ ַ ּי ֲעבוֹ ר יְ הֹוָ ה ַעל ּ ָפנָ יו וַ ִ ּי ְק ָרא‬
And Hashem passed before Moshe and Hashem called out (that we should say the
following in our tefillos):

.‫יְ הֹוָ ה‬
He is compassionate before one has sinned. We need Hashem’s mercy even before
we ever sin, for Hashem owes us nothing! All that we have is only due to His mercy
and compassion.

.‫יְ הֹוָ ה‬
He is compassionate after one has sinned and done teshuvah. Unlike human
beings, who tend to distance themselves from those who have wronged them,
Hashem’s closeness to us remains even after we have sinned (even seriously)
against Him.

.‫ֵאל‬
Hashem compassionately uses His might to restrain His anger and arouse
His mercy.

.‫ַרחוּם‬
He eases the punishment of sinners when they call out to Him.

.‫וְ ַח ּנוּן‬
He acts charitably to those who ask for it, even if they are unworthy.

.‫ֶא ֶר ְך ַא ּ ַפיִ ם‬

He is slow to anger and does not punish us immediately — in the hope that we
will improve.

.‫וְ ַרב ֶח ֶסד‬
He shows great chessed to those who need it but do not necessarily deserve it by
their own merit.

.‫וֶ ֱא ֶמת‬
He is faithful to generously reward those who do His will.

.‫נ ֵֹצר ֶח ֶסד ָל ֲא ָל ִפים‬
He extends the reward for mitzvos that a person does for two thousand
generations (for the benefit of his descendants).

.‫ֹשא ָעוֹן‬
ֵׂ ‫נ‬
He forgives deliberate sins.

.‫וָ ֶפ ׁ ַשע‬
He forgives rebellious sins.

.‫וְ ַח ָּט ָאה‬
He forgives unintentional sins.

:‫וְ נַ ֵּקה‬
He cleanses those who do teshuvah and gives them a fresh start!

:ּ‫אתנ ּו וּנְ ַח ְל ָּתנו‬
ֵ ‫וְ ָס ַל ְח ָּת ַל ֲעוֹנֵ נ ּו ו ְּל ַח ָּט‬
‫ ִּכי ַא ָּתה‬,ּ‫ ְמ ַחל ָלנ ּו ַמ ְל ֵּכנ ּו ִּכי ָפ ׁ ָש ְענו‬,ּ‫ְס ַלח ָלנ ּו ָא ִבינ ּו ִּכי ָח ָטאנו‬

Please forgive our sins — and keep us as Your special and eternal nation — for You
are full of kindness to all who call to You.

A SPECIAL TIME, SPECIAL REQUESTS

‫אבינו מלכנו‬
On fast days (except Tishah B’Av) and during the Aseres Yemei Teshuvah, we add
Avinu Malkeinu before Tachanun:

Is Hashem our kind and compassionate Father (‫ )אבינו‬Who understands our
failings, or is He our King (‫ )מלכנו‬with demands and rules? The answer is — He
is both! Hashem is our King, with a special mission for each of us, with rules
and structures to help us maximize our spiritual potential to fulfill that mission.
And we must never lose sight of our responsibilities and the end goal — those
highest rungs of the ladder we climb in our avodas Hashem. Our decisions do
have very serious consequences!
Yet, importantly, He is also our Father Who understands that at times we
stumble, lose our footing, and get sidetracked from our goal. Through all
this, He still supports us and gives us multiple opportunities to get back on
track. On these special days, when we think about who we really are, what our
mission is, what our real potential is, and take stock of our progress, we need
to focus on both those aspects of our relationship with Him. By doing that, we
will be worthy of Hashem giving us what we are asking for so that we can get
back up and be the people He created us to be.

79 Shacharis • Avinu Malkeinu
NOTE: The Hebrew of this tefillah is quite simple and straightforward. I urge you to say each line slowly
in the original.

Instant Insight:
Regarding the
“book of merits”
or remembering us
“for merits”: Either
one has merits or
they do not! If they
do, they are in the
book; and if not,
what is Hashem
supposed to do?
What does this
mean? The idea
here is that Hashem
could arrange your
life so that it’s easy
for you to pick up

We are asking

remember us and
write us in the

will have many

mitzvos!

NOTE:
The general custom
is that women do
not say Tachanun
(neither the
“long Tachanun”
nor the “short
Tachanun”). Why?
The most common
explanation is
that Tachanun,
similar to Maariv,
was originally an
optional tefillah
that women did
not accept upon
themselves.
FAQ:
But why? Why
the differences
in tefillah (and
practices) between
men and women?
ANSWER:
Very common
and complicated
question! See
Appendix 11.

‫תחנון‬
I Am Not Worthy, and We Are Not Worthy, but We Are
Asking for Divine Favor — Personally and Nationally

‫ ואנחנו לא נדע‬/ ‫ שומר ישראל‬/ ‫ ויאמר דוד‬/ ‫והוא רחום‬
For a list of when Tachanun is omitted, see Appendix 12:9.
There is a very tricky and delicate balance needed in proper,
healthy avodas Hashem, which is reflected in our tefillos. On
one hand, we need to admit (to Hashem and to ourselves!)
that we have made mistakes and are not completely living
up to our potential. In terms of strict justice and the letter
of the law, we are not really worthy of all the bounty that
Hashem gives us. There are even times that we use His gifts
against Him! And that is the major focus of Tachanun. But on
the other hand, the goal of saying Tachanun is not to give
up and say, “OK—so I’m just a big loser—forget it!” As we
explained earlier, by Modeh Ani—clearly Hashem believes in
you! He woke you up. He believes you are important and
that you CAN correct your mistakes in order to reach greater
heights! But if you avoid confronting your mistakes and tell
yourself, “I’m doing just fine and I have nothing to improve,”
you’ll never aspire to be the greatest “you” you can be. Be
honest about your faults, and then believe in yourself as
much as Hashem believes in you!
“Long Tachanun” (only on Mondays and Thursdays)
On all other weekdays, begin with "Short Tachanun" on page 87.
Why Mondays and Thursdays? These two days are
considered “‫ — עת רצון‬favorable days,” since Moshe went
up to Har Sinai on a Thursday (the first of Elul) to get the
second set of Luchos and came down forty days later on
a Monday (on Yom Kippur itself) with the second set of
Luchos. Since they are auspicious days, we add in these
seven paragraphs of Tachanun.

‫שחרית • תחנון‬

84

What is the theme? These seven paragraphs were written by different sages
and eventually were put together to form the “long Tachanun,” which is why
you will find that many of the ideas and themes repeat themselves. The basic
theme is that Hashem is our only hope for salvation from the suffering that
we have brought upon ourselves through not living up to our mission to the
world. As contrasted with the “short Tachanun,” which is personal in nature
(and said every weekday), the “long Tachanun” focuses on our communal
pain, and the desecrated status of Yerushalayim, our holy city.
Since the wording of the seven paragraphs of “long Tachanun” is quite
straightforward and understandable, we will simply point out the key themes
of each paragraph.

‫וְ הוּא ַרחוּם יְ ַכ ּ ֵפר ָעוֹן וְ לֹא‬
ֹ‫ וְ ִה ְר ָּבה ְל ָה ׁ ִשיב ַא ּפו‬,‫יַ ׁ ְש ִחית‬
,‫ ַא ָּתה יְ הֹוָ ה‬: ֹ‫וְ לֹא יָ ִעיר ָּכל ֲח ָמתו‬
ָ ‫לֹא ִת ְכ ָלא ַר ֲח ֶמ‬
‫ ַח ְסדְּ ָך‬,ּ‫יך ִמ ֶּמ ּנו‬
‫יענ ּו‬
ֵ ‫הוש‬
ִׁ
:ּ‫וַ ֲא ִמ ְּת ָך ָּת ִמיד יִ ְ ּצרוּנו‬
,‫יְ הֹוָ ה ֱאל ֵֹהינ ּו וְ ַק ְּב ֵצנ ּו ִמן ַהגּ וֹ יִ ם‬
ָ
‫ ִאם ֲעוֹנוֹ ת‬:‫ ְל ִה ׁ ְש ַּת ֵּב ַח ִּב ְת ִה ָּל ֶתך‬,‫ְלהוֹ דוֹ ת ְל ׁ ֵשם ָק ְד ׁ ֶש ָך‬
‫ לֹא‬:‫יחה ְל ַמ ַען ִּת ָּו ֵרא‬
ָ ‫ ִּכי ִע ְּמ ָך ַה ּ ְס ִל‬:‫ ֲאדֹנָ י ִמי יַ ֲעמֹד‬,‫ִּת ׁ ְש ָמר יָ ּה‬
‫ ִאם ֲעוֹנֵ ינ ּו‬:ּ‫שה ָלנ ּו וְ לֹא ַכ ֲעוֹנוֹ ֵתינ ּו ִּתגְ מֹל ָע ֵלינו‬
ׂ ֶ ‫ַכ ֲח ָט ֵאינ ּו ַּת ֲע‬
ָ‫ וַ ֲח ָס ֶדיך‬,‫יך יְ הֹוָ ה‬
ָ ‫ זְ כֹר ַר ֲח ֶמ‬:‫שה ְל ַמ ַען ׁ ְש ֶמ ָך‬
ׂ ֵ ‫ ֲע‬,‫ יְ הֹוָ ה‬,ּ‫ָענ ּו ָבנו‬

ׂ ַ ְ‫ י‬,‫ יַ ֲענֵ נ ּו יְ הֹוָ ה ְּביוֹ ם ָצ ָרה‬:‫ִּכי ֵמעוֹ ָלם ֵה ָּמה‬
‫ ָא ִבינ ּו ַמ ְל ֵּכנ ּו‬:ּ‫ ַה ֶּמ ֶל ְך יַ ֲענֵ נ ּו ְּביוֹ ם ָק ְר ֵאנו‬,‫יעה‬
ָ ‫ יְ הֹוָ ה הוֹ ׁ ִש‬:‫יַ ֲעקֹב‬
ָ:‫שה ִע ָּמנ ּו ְל ַמ ַען ׁ ְש ֶמך‬

‫ — וְהּוא ַרחּום י ְַכּפֵר ָעֹון‬We admit that
it is because of our own sins that
we, Your nation, and Your holy
city Yerushalayim have become an
object of scorn among the nations
(which is a huge chillul Hashem). We
ask that You save us and Your city
in the merit of our ancestors and
because Your kindness is eternal.

‫אתי ְל ָפנֶיָך‬
ִ ‫ְחּנּון ָח ָט‬
ַ ‫ — ַרחּום ו‬I know I have
sinned, but I ask, please, Hashem,
do not punish me “out of anger,” but
rather with love, in a way that Your
punishments will help me see my
mistakes and be a catalyst to correct
them! At times, I feel so weighed
down with troubles and with a feeling
that I have missed opportunities and
not lived up to my potential! But I
am confident that You will accept
my tefillah, give me opportunities to
improve, and rid me of my difficulties,
so that I can grow and serve You to my
fullest potential — which is why You
put me here on earth!

On Mondays and Thursdays (when the “long Tachanun” focuses on our communal
suffering and pain), add the following; on other days, continue on page 90.

‫וח ֶלת‬

ֲ ‫( — ו‬To sum it all up:)
Hashem, we do not know what else
we can do, so we turn to You for
Your kindness and help. We know
we lack merit, but You know that
we are material beings and thus
have many struggles! Pardon us
and give us another chance to prove
ourselves, so that we may spread
the knowledge of Your Presence to
the whole world.

‫סדר קריאת התורה‬
Now Let’s Learn Some Torah!
Everyone should learn Torah every day — each at their own level! In addition to
this, a public reading of the Torah was established for Mondays and Thursdays
as a mini “reenactment” of Maamad Har Sinai, so that we may reaffirm the
Torah as being our guide — not only personally but also nationally.
Taking Out the Torah

‫ גדלו‬/ ‫ בריך שמה‬/ ‫ ויהי בנסוע הארון‬/ ‫אל ארך אפים‬

You are the Source of everything!
Please redeem us, rebuild the
Beis Hamikdash, shine Your light
on us, and accept all our tefillos.
May we all live long and happy
lives. Protect me, all that is mine,
and all that belongs to all of Bnei
Yisrael. You feed all, You sustain all,
You rule over all — and I am Your
servant, who bows before Your
glorious Torah! I do not put my
trust in humans or in angels, but
rather, I place my complete trust
in You, for You are the One True
God, Whose Torah and Prophets
are all true, and Whose actions are
all good and true! Please, open my
heart to Your Torah! And fulfill all
that I and all Your nation Yisrael
wish for — but only if what we wish
for is really good for us and will give
us life and peace!

‫סדר ברכת התורה‬
Brachah before an aliyah:

:‫) ָּב ְרכ ּו ֶאת יְ הֹוָ ה ַה ְמב ָֹר ְך‬:‫(העולה אומר‬
‫( — ָּב ְרכּו ֶאת ְיהֹוָה ַה ְמב ָֹרְך‬The one receiving the aliyah calls out to us:) We must make the
world realize (by how we speak and how we conduct ourselves daily) that Hashem is
the Source of all, and that everything He does is perfect and for the ultimate best.

:‫) ָּברו ְּך יְ הֹוָ ה ַה ְמב ָֹר ְך ְלעוֹ ָלם וָ ֶעד‬:‫(והקהל עונין‬
:‫) ָּברו ְּך יְ הֹוָ ה ַה ְמב ָֹר ְך ְלעוֹ ָלם וָ ֶעד‬:‫(והעולה חוזר‬
‫ָעד‬
ֶ ‫עֹולם ו‬
ָ ‫( — ָּברּוְך ְיהֹוָה ַה ְמב ָֹרְך ְל‬and we respond:) Yes, we pledge to make the world
realize (by how we speak and how we conduct ourselves daily) that Hashem is the
Source of all, and that everything He does is perfect and for the ultimate best, for
all eternity!

‫ָּברו ְּך ַא ָּתה יְ הֹוָ ה‬
‫ ֲא ׁ ֶשר‬,‫ֱאל ֵֹהינ ּו ֶמ ֶל ְך ָהעוֹ ָלם‬
‫ וְ נָ ַתן‬,‫ָּב ַחר ָּבנ ּו ִמ ָּכל ָה ַע ִּמים‬
‫ ָּברו ְּך ַא ָּתה‬: ֹ‫ָלנ ּו ֶאת ּתוֹ ָרתו‬
:‫ נוֹ ֵתן ַה ּתוֹ ָרה‬,‫יְ הֹוָ ה‬

(The one receiving the aliyah repeats
):‫(ואחר כך מברך‬
the above and continues:)
‫ — ָּברּוְך ַא ָּתה ְיהֹוָה‬You, Hashem,
are the Source of everything;
‫ֹלהינּו‬
ֵ ‫ — ֱא‬You take care of us; ‫ֶמ ֶלְך‬
‫עֹולם‬
ָ ‫ — ָה‬and You rule the entire
world; ‫( ֲא ֶׁשר ָּב ַחר ָּבנּו ִמ ָּכל ָה ַע ִּמים‬we are
so happy that) You have chosen us,
Bnei Yisrael, out of all the nations
of the world; ‫ּתֹורתֹו‬
ָ ‫ָתן ָלנּו ֶאת‬
ַ ‫ — ְונ‬and given us Your Torah. ‫ — ָּברּוְך ַא ָּתה ְיהֹוָה‬You,
Hashem, are the Source of everything; ‫ּתֹורה‬
ָ ‫נֹותן ַה‬
ֵ — Who gives (us) the Torah.
Brachah at the end of the aliyah:

‫ָּברו ְּך ַא ָּתה יְ הֹוָ ה ֱאל ֵֹהינ ּו‬
