Instant Insight:
By all other brachos
of the Shemoneh
Esrei, we simply
respond “Amen”
to the chazzan’s
brachah. Why
here must we say
our own “Modim,”
as if an Amen is
not sufficient? All
other brachos are
requests. When you
are with someone
else and they
put in a request
for something
important, saying
simply, “And me
too — I also agree!” is
fine. He represents
you. That is Amen.
But if someone is
really bursting with
gratitude, especially
to Hashem, for
“our lives, which
are entrusted into
Your Hand” and
“for our miraculous
survival till today,”
then Amen is
impossible. No one
can “represent”
your inner feeling
of gratitude! It must
burst forth! Thus,
Modim D’Rabbanan!

(hidden) miracles that are with us every day (and we do not even
realize it!); and for Your wonders and Your acts of goodness that
occur at all times; evening, morning, and afternoon (literally,
twenty-four hours a day, as well as figuratively, during the dark
times in our lives, the clear bright times, and the hazy twilight times).

compassion is never-ending; ‫ ִּכי ֹלא ַתּמּו ֲח ָסדֶיָך‬,‫ְה ְּמ ַר ֵחם‬
ַ ‫ — ו‬You are
the merciful One, for Your loving-kindness is never exhausted.

hope in You.

Instant Insight:
Generally, we think
of “‫ — שלום‬peace”
as meaning people
getting along with
each other. But if
so, then the third
brachah should
have said “‫וישם‬
‫ — לכם שלום‬Hashem
will give peace to
you,” plural, i.e., to
the group. Why is
the word “‫ — לך‬to
you,” singular,
used? The singular
is used because
you cannot get
along with others
if you first do not
get along with
yourself! Thus this
brachah is to the
individual — that
each person should
have inner serenity
and peace. Only
then can we live in
peace with others.

also gave us a love of doing kindness and righteousness. (This all leads to everyone
sharing) their blessings with each other, living with compassion, and thus having a
full life and living in peace (by forgiving others).

APPENDICES

239 A Personal Note

A PERSONAL NOTE
FROM RABBI FEIGENBAUM FOR
PARENTS, MECHANCHIM, AND
MECHANCHOS
Teens—see page 240 for YOUR personal note!

“Are you sure my kid/student should be reading this? I grew up fine
without all this stuff!”
Me too! I went to school in the ’50s and ’60s, and I don’t remember
asking any of these questions! In addition, as you are well
aware, today there are a plethora of books on the overall topic of
emunah — how to approach it, if to approach it, intellectual analysis,
appeals to the emotions, self-awareness, new-age and old-age, and
so on! In addition, there are many books and sefarim that deal with
very specific issues of Torah hashkafah, and many of these works
are heavily footnoted, showing a level of erudition of Rishonim
and Acharonim that surpasses my knowledge base. So what are this
siddur and appendix doing?
I am basing myself on my unique area of expertise — the frum
teenager. In order to pasken any issue properly, one needs to be
an expert in the reality of the issue. And as far as the reality of
teenagers goes, I have merited over the past forty years to have many
DMCs (deep and meaningful conversations) with your mainstream
“good” frum yeshiva bachurim/Bais Yaakov girls from all over North
America, England, and Israel. Many of these DMCs have been in
person and many have been by email. And I’m not talking about
those who might be termed “messed-up” or “off-the-derech” hanging
out on street corners. Those kids are doing fine! They are being
honest with where they are holding, and we have invested financial
and human resources to create programs to educate them, validate
them, embrace them, and thus get them back. I am talking instead
about the “good” kids — the “don’t ask, don’t tell,” or the “walk the
walk, talk the talk” kids. Those are the ones who are either acting
out somewhat (or a lot), but under the radar, or who are not acting
out but are simply unexcited — either way, they are full of questions.
In every community, in every “style” of education, no matter how
sheltered and heimishe or large and metropolitan, no matter “in town”
or “out of town” — they are all the same! They’re all asking the same
questions and all share the same nagging concerns. And that is fine!
As Harav Wolbe, zt”l, would say, “There is no such thing as an
apikorsus question — only apikorsus answers.” The only difference is

A Personal Note

that the good kids are street-smart enough to keep those questions
to themselves in order not to become a “case.”
Harav Aharon Feldman, shlita, Rosh Yeshivas Ner Israel of Baltimore,
summed it up very succinctly at a Torah Umesorah convention when
he said, “We are raising a generation of young people who are very
observant, but not very religious.”
The opening essay (“But I Don’t Do Davening”) and the following
answers to the questions were written for teens — not adults! Adults
are working with different assumptions, have a very different
knowledge base, have different life experiences (for better or for
worse), and adults and teens use different words to mean different
things (we do not speak the same language!). Simply put, adults and
teens read things with different glasses. If the following makes sense
to you and helps, great! If not, then get one of the many books written
for adults. And if this will generate more questions in your son or
daughter or students — then ashreichem! You will have a wonderful
opportunity to fulfill “v’shinantam l’vanecha” in a meaningful way!
Much hatzlachah!

A PERSONAL NOTE FROM
RABBI FEIGENBAUM FOR TEENS
I assume you read the introduction for the adults! Before getting
to the answers, I want to make three very important points about
questions. As I said above, questions are fine. As Harav Wolbe, zt”l,
would say, “There is no such thing as an apikorsus question — only
apikorsus answers.” But (there always is a “but”) …
1. As much as we all wish our daily experience of Torah and mitzvos
to be understandable, uplifting, meaningful, and exciting, the
truth is that for most people, at times in our lives, it is not! Still,
the bedrock of our emunah is that Hashem spoke at Har Sinai
and chose us for a mission (which is borne out by our miraculous
existence until today, which makes no sense — much to the
consternation of the rest of the world!), and therefore, simply
put, we must get with the program and do the best we can! And
that is the first brachah of our daily Birchos HaTorah, which is
specifically “la’asok b’divrei Sorah” — you just have to do it! You
have questions? Fine. Good! Understanding and connecting more
is great. The second brachah is “v’haarev na” — that Torah should
be sweet and understandable; that is our goal! However, it can
take years and years to ask all the questions and get the answers.
Until that happens, we must start with la’asok — to get with the

240

241 A Personal Note

program and just do it as well as we can. As we said inside the
siddur: “Seichel tov” — A good understanding of Torah is given,
“l’chol oseihem” — to those who practice it! So you should certainly
ask questions, but keep “doing Torah” while you figure things out.
2. Your questions must be honest questions and not excuses.
Meaning, you need to be honest with yourself. Are you involved
in inappropriate stuff and you need an excuse so that you don’t
feel guilty? (“Hey, it’s OK to do x, y, z because this stuff doesn’t
make sense anyways!”) Is that why you are asking your questions?
If that is your motivation, then don’t waste your time. People who
do that read this section with the goal of not liking the answers
in order to say to themselves, “See, I read the book, I’m still not
convinced, so now I can go and mess up my life.” I once spoke
to a young lady who told me, “I’m working on making sure that
Yiddishkeit is not true, because if it is, I am doomed to a life of
gloom and doom!” I felt bad that her experience of Torah and
mitzvos was so negative, but she wasn’t asking questions, so I
couldn’t give her answers! So try to be honest and aim high. If
you’re like most teens I’ve met — honest, growing, sincere young
people with neshamos thirsting for some clarity — then hopefully
this will help a bit.
3. These are all complex topics. Entire books have been written on
each of these questions. My goal here is to give some basic sense
of how to approach these topics and give enough clarity that
these questions will not get in the way of your davening. But
there is much more to these topics than what is written here. If
these answers raise even more questions, great! Go find books
on the subject, ask your rebbeim and morahs, or email me at
rabbiyfeigenbaum@gmail.com, and I will try to clarify things.
Thanks for listening, and may you have much success in your growth
to becoming a quality, contributing, and confident ben Torah/
bas Yisrael.

Appendix 1

242

APPENDIX 1
HOW AND WHY DAVENING WORKS
AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES
We must first explain some crucial hashkafah concepts in order to
answer these questions. (Sorry, some questions do not have short
answers. And no, you cannot skip this introduction and go straight to
the answers! And — you must do the questions in order!)
Rav Dessler (Michtav Me’Eliyahu, Kuntres Ha’bechirah, chelek 4)
introduces us to the fundamental concept of mazal elyon and mazal
tachton, by first giving us a deep understanding of the term “mazal.”
Rav Dessler explains that mazal, which comes from the word “nozel”
(liquid or flow), refers to all the stuff that Hashem “pours down” on
you from birth (e.g., where you were born, when you were born, who
your parents are, your talents and handicaps — both physical and
emotional) and all the stuff that happens in life (e.g., the people He
arranges you to meet, winning lotteries, chas v’shalom getting cancer,
etc.). All these things are the tools He gives you so you can fulfill
your many particular jobs on this earth. Perhaps your job is to be
that poor person who makes the correct choice to be happy with
what he has and be an inspiration to others (rather than making the
incorrect choice of being cranky and angry). In addition, poor people
provide others with the opportunity to do the mitzvah of tzedakah.
Someone needs to be poor; without poor people, how would everyone
else be challenged between an evening watching dumb videos or
volunteering for Tomchei Shabbos? Needing Tomchei Shabbos
packages is an important job — and Hashem chose you!
Of course, someone also needs to be rich so they’ll have to deal with
deciding whether to waste five thousand dollars on a watch no one
will ever see or to give that amount to Tomchei Shabbos. That’s
also an important job! Same goes with being sick or healthy, having
stable parents or growing up in a dysfunctional home, or being the
kid who tutors others or the one who gets tutored! Someone needs
to be the person who inspires everyone (or not!) by losing their
job and downsizing their house while smiling and saying, “Gam zu
l’tovah — It’s all for the best!” Similarly, someone needs to suddenly

Introduction

243 Appendix 1

make a fortune and remain humble and keep his same, simple
lifestyle (or not!). You get the idea.
FAQ: But some people seem to have a really “fun”
mazal — brains, money, a happy home, good looks, and/
or an easygoing nature — while others seem to have
a really lousy “not fun” mazal — learning disabilities,
messed-up home, physical disabilities, and/or an
awkward personality. That seems not fair!
It’s a famous question — and a good one! See Appendix 2 below, but
first continue reading!
Rav Dessler asks: Can one daven to change their mazal? How would
that sound?
“Hashem, I think I’ve been doing a really good job with the mazal
you gave me. I’m here in the hospital, I smile, I say ‘gam zu l’tovah,’ I
cheer up other patients, I’m nice to all the people who are using me
as their chessed case and are really nudniks, but I pretend to be happy
to see them. How about switching things up so that I become that
person who miraculously recovers and still doesn’t take things for
granted, and inspires others, etc.? That’s also an important job; can
I now have that one?”
Rav Dessler quotes what seem to be contradictory answers from
Chazal (you can see all those quotes if you look up Kuntres Ha’bechirah,
chelek 4) and resolves the seeming contradiction by explaining that
there are two kinds of mazal:
• One is mazal elyon. That’s all the stuff you were given as part
of your “jobs” and challenges on earth. Hopefully, you’ll make
the correct decisions, do the best that Hashem knows you can
under the circumstances, do whatever tikkun is needed, increase
kedushah in the world, and earn Olam Haba for that! Or you’ll mess
up, not do what Hashem knows you could have done, decrease
kedushah, and earn Gehinnom. It’s your choice; you have free will.
The stuff that you were born with, or that happens along the
way in life, cannot be changed — they’re what you’re out here for;
they are your jobs! Deal with it. You get what you get and you say,
“Thank you”! That’s mazal elyon. (“But my mazal elyon really stinks
compared to that other kid’s!” Relax; we’ll deal with that later.)
• But a lot of stuff is not part of your main job but is rather stuff
that exists to make your main jobs easier (or harder) to handle.
Being sick in the hospital is a difficult “job,” but it’s easier if you
have money. Being poor is a difficult “job,” but it’s easier if you
are healthy. That secondary, “not main job” kind of stuff is mazal
tachton and is changeable! Hashem starts you off with your mazal
elyon jobs, and based on how you’re doing, He can make the mazal

Appendix 1

244

tachton stuff easier or worse! How do we get Hashem to make our
mazal tachton stuff better? Well, certainly by doing our mazal elyon
job well, doing mitzvos, and, most importantly, by talking to Him!
Every time you turn to Hashem and show Him (and yourself)
that you realize He is in total control, it raises your spiritual level,
your neshamah gets a bit more pure, you are a different person
than yesterday, and that makes you possibly deserve a better
mazal tachton.
“Well, duh — I know that Hashem is in control, and He knows I know
that, so what do I have to say it for?!” Not everyone realizes, but
there’s a big difference between “I have it in my head” as contrasted
to verbalizing it — getting it out in the open — and actually talking
directly to Hashem and creating a relationship.
So, if you’re asking for changes in stuff that’s your mazal elyon, then
obviously Hashem must say no. That’s your job! On the other hand,
if what you’re asking for relates to mazal tachton stuff, then possibly,
if you deserve it, Hashem could say yes to your request.
“But how are we supposed to know what stuff in our lives is mazal
elyon and what is mazal tachton?” We don’t!
“Rabbi Feigenbaum, that’s not funny! How do we know what we can
daven for and what we can’t?” Well, Rav Dessler brings a story from
the Gemara about Rav Elazar ben Pedas who constantly davened to
get out of his poverty. Hashem spoke to him and told him that it
simply won’t happen since poverty is his mazal elyon. But assuming
you’re not on the level of Rav Pedas — meaning that Hashem doesn’t
talk to you directly — then you don’t know!
“So what do I do?” You daven! You show Hashem that you accept
that He is ultimately in control, you create a relationship with Him,
you thank Him for what you do have, and thus you raise your own
spiritual level and your own kedushah. If Hashem says yes to your
requests, great! If He chooses not to, also great!
“But if He decides to turn down my requests since they refer to my
mazal elyon, what happens to all those tefillos, kabbalos, or extra
chessed I did to deserve a ‘yes’ answer?” They don’t go to waste! You
earned reward, and this will perhaps go for some mazal tachton areas
or for Olam Haba (that’s not a bad thing, you know!).
How does tefillah work? By turning to Hashem and showing Him that
you appreciate what He has given you, and you accept that He is in
control, you bring yourself closer to Him and raise your spiritual level.
The same is true when you notch up the quality of your avodas Hashem.
Today you are a very different “you” than yesterday — more spiritual
and with a better balance sheet of mitzvos versus aveiros. And thus
perhaps the “you” of today is deserving of a different mazal tachton.

Summarize

245 Appendix 1

FAQ: But how does it work if I daven for someone else,
especially someone so sick they cannot even daven for
themselves? He or she is not doing any extra mitzvah
that would make them worthy of a different mazal.
Great question. Let’s answer with a mashal: Your friend, who is a bit
selfish, wants to stay home Thursday night and just do nothing. You
convince him to come along with you and do some chessed. He gets
the mitzvah of chessed and gets rewarded for that. His neshamah is
now a bit more kadosh. What about you? Besides the chessed that you
also did, you get an additional reward for getting someone else to do
a mitzvah! Every time you cause someone to do a mitzvah (or stop
them from doing something inappropriate), you get reward. That
itself raises your neshamah! So the person you are davening for also
got people to do mitzvos. How did he do that? By being sick! Because
of him, you said an extra tefillah or gave a little extra tzedakah.
And that sick person gets rewarded for that, which then raises his
neshamah. And if his sickness is his mazal tachton, that may just push
him over the threshold and he will deserve to get better! (Of course,
if it’s his mazal elyon, then he still gets the reward, but it will be used
for something else, like we said above.)

Now Let’s Get
to the FAQs

‫ — שעשה לי כל צרכי‬If that’s true, then why should we ask
Hashem for stuff? He already gave me all I need!
Yes, He did give you all you need! And one of the things you “need”
is to create a relationship with Hashem and talk to Him on a regular
basis! What was the curse of the snake? “V’afar tochal kol yemei
chayecha — Dust you will eat all the days of your life.” The snake eats
dust. Why is that a curse? Hashem is giving him all his food forever,
everywhere, and at any time! The answer is that this itself is the
curse! The snake never needs to turn to Hashem; Hashem wants
nothing to do with him. “Here, take your stuff; go away and don’t
bother Me ever again!” That is the greatest curse.
“But if Hashem would just give me everything I wanted right away,
I’d still talk to Him regularly to thank Him for my great life!” Oh,
sure! Be honest! That is very unlikely. We very quickly take the good
for granted. And Hashem knows that too. Therefore, Hashem creates
situations that push us to talk to Him in order to help us create a
relationship with Him.
FAQ: Why am I asking Hashem for stuff? Hashem knows
what I want, and He also knows what I really need! If
I really need it and am supposed to have it, He would
have given it to me!

Appendix 1

FAQ: Hashem knows I am sick and knows I obviously
want to get better! If He wanted me healed, He would
have healed me without me asking!
Nope! Perhaps He wants you to stretch yourself spiritually! Hashem
started you with a certain mazal tachton, and now you need to raise
your spiritual level through tefillah to deserve that change! As we said
above, some things that you need to help make your mazal elyon job
easier you must earn on your own! Thus you may need to be healed
(and Hashem knows that), but you will only get it if you deserve it.
And as we said, the best way to earn it is by raising your spiritual
level through actually taking the time to verbalize what you want and
having a sincere and heartfelt discussion with Hashem.
FAQ: Why should I bother making requests from
Hashem? I’ve asked Him many times before for stuff,
and He keeps on saying no!
FAQ: Hashem “heals the sick”? No, He doesn’t! I’ve
asked to be healed many times, and He still says no!
Right! And there are many reasons that Hashem may choose to turn
down your request. As humans, it’s a little silly to think that we
can understand all of Hashem’s calculations! (See Appendix 2 and a
super mashal of this.) Perhaps He said no because you weren’t sincere
or worthy enough, so you must try harder. Perhaps you were truly
sincere, but if you could see a video of what you were like on this earth
three hundred years ago, you’d understand very well why all this is
happening! We do believe in the concept of gilgul, reincarnation. The
Ramchal in Derech Hashem (the chapter “Hashgachah Ishis — Individual
Providence”) goes on at length about the fact that our actions in
earlier gilgulim could have an affect on the mazal of our present gilgul.
And since you and I have no way of knowing that information, there
will be times when we simply cannot figure out why Hashem is saying
no to our requests. Perhaps, somehow the experience of this struggle
is important for you in the future. When the time comes, you’ll look
back and say, “Aha! Now I get it”! (And then you can print it in a book
of hashgachah pratis stories or be part of some inspiring video!) And
of course, as we said above, perhaps what you’re requesting is simply
your mazal elyon and therefore unchangeable. Since you never know,
you keep asking (hopefully, with a smile on your face and without
frustration), and that raises your neshamah no matter what, earns
you reward, adds kedushah to the world, and brings Mashiach a bit
closer. And that’s not a bad thing!
“But Rabbi Feigenbaum, my life, and the whole world, just looks so
haphazard!”
True — next question!

246

247 Appendix 2

APPENDIX 2
TZADDIK V’RA LO

FAQ: Thank you? For what? My life stinks!
FAQ: What? It certainly doesn’t look like Hashem
“provides all of us with all we need”! There are people
out there really suffering!
FAQ: But how am I supposed to now sing all these
praises to Hashem when my life is so difficult? I have so
much stuff going on, and it’s hard to accept that all is
fair and just!
FAQ: What? It certainly doesn’t look like Hashem “does
good to everyone”! There are people out there really
suffering! And my life is a mess! I certainly don’t see
“prosperity, success, and relief from our troubles”!
FAQ: “Never did I see a tzaddik who was forsaken (by
Hashem), whose children had to go begging for food.”
But that’s not what it looks like!
This is the famous question of “tzaddik v’ra lo” — why some
(seemingly) good people have such lousy lives. And it does appear to
us that Hashem has not divided up the mazal elyon very fairly among
people, as we asked above. To be honest, this is a very complex
topic, which is really for another time and another book — im yirtzeh
Hashem. This is not the place for a lengthy discussion as to how
Hashem makes His decisions, neither is this the place to prove to you
that clearly Hashem has a plan that makes sense and that He runs
the world with careful forethought and planning (“HaTzur tamim
pa’alo ki chol derachav mishpat — All that Hashem does is perfect, for
all His paths are just”). All that is for another time.
But I know that this topic is very painful, and for some people (teens
and adults) it makes davening and turning to Hashem very difficult.
So I will try to at least answer the following question: “I accept that
Hashem has an intelligent plan for all that happens. But I am not
stupid! If there is such an intelligent plan, how come I can’t see it?
How come to us it seems to make no sense, if it supposedly really
does make sense?”

Appendix 2 248

The answer to that is the following mashal. It’s not mine! I heard it
sourced to Rav Elchonon Wasserman and Rav Yitzchok Hutner. It’s
long, but read it carefully, as it explains a lot.
Imagine a person who grew up in the city and has no idea where food
comes from. He just goes to the supermarket and sees bread on the
shelves every day and buys a loaf without thinking at all how bread is
manufactured or what ingredients are used to create it. It’s just there
like everything else in the store!
One day, he takes a drive out to the countryside. He sees a nice empty
plot of land and thinks to himself that this plot of land is nice and
smooth and could be used for building a home. All of a sudden, he
sees some person dragging some kind of huge knife up and down
the land — completely messing it up! Now it’s ugly mud! And even
crazier, this person is dropping little “things” into this mud. He goes
over to check it out, picks up one of those little things and realizes
they are edible. He thinks to himself, “What a bad person! He turns
a wonderful piece of land into mud and then throws yummy seeds
into it!” This guy must be evil or crazy! Our city slicker decides to
wait and see what happens. After a while (use your imagination!),
he sees pretty, brownish “flowers” growing out of the ground. Now
he changes his mind and says to himself, “Oy! I was all wrong. This
person is not some evil guy who throws food into the mud. He’s a
good person who grows pretty flowers.”
Just as he is about to leave, he sees the man cutting down those
flowers. “Oh, he’s probably going to give them to someone,” thinks
our city person. But then he sees that man grind those pretty flowers
into a powder! “This guy is an evil maniac!” says our city person to
himself. “First I thought he was a bad person for messing up a nice
field. Then I thought he was even worse for throwing away yummy
seeds. Then I thought I misjudged him and that, really, he was a good
person since he was growing pretty flowers to give to someone. Now
I realize that he is even more crazy and evil than I thought in the
beginning! He grows flowers and then wantonly destroys them! That
is nuts! I better get out of here!”
Just as he is about to leave, he sees the man pouring water and other
stuff into that powder. He goes over to check it out. He sticks his
finger in and tastes it — delicious!
“What is that,” he asks the man, “and what are you?” The man tells
him that he is a farmer, and that stuff is called dough. “Amazing, this
stuff is great,” says the man. And now he is ready to leave, and he
thinks to himself the following: “What a fool I was! I never understood
anything! What I thought was bad — destroying the land, grinding
the flowers — was not bad. And what I thought was good — growing
pretty flowers — was not good! It was all part of a process to make

249 Appendix 2

this stuff called dough. Now I understand everything! It all now
makes sense.”
As he gets into his car, he sees the farmer take that yummy dough,
roll it into a ball, and throw it into a fire! “What?! That is completely
crazy, random, evil, and senseless! Now absolutely nothing at all that
I have seen makes any sense at all. Why would anyone work that hard
on something so wonderful and throw it into a fire? That farmer guy
must be a maniac.”
And then he sees a loaf of bread come out of the oven! Now he realizes
that he actually never understood anything. What he thought was
good was not really good. And what he thought was bad was not really
bad. Of course, since he never knew where bread comes from and
never knew the process, he had no way of imagining how grinding
“pretty flowers” could have any relationship to the bread he buys in
the supermarket. And had he not stayed until the end, he would have
been positive he understood everything, when, of course, he hadn’t!
That is us in this world. As humans, we don’t know the “process,” and
we certainly can’t see the end product! That will only happen when
Mashiach comes. What we think now is the “good” may not be, and
certainly what appears to be the “bad” may not be bad. We are just
like that city person. We see just part of something; we don’t know
how the process works, and we can’t see the end of six thousand
years of history. We do know that Hashem told us that all is just.
We have also seen the miraculous survival of Bnei Yisrael. Therefore,
when we encounter something we don’t understand, we say, “Gam zu
l’tovah — It is all for the best.”

Appendix 3 250

APPENDIX 3
THE POSITIVE POWER
OF MAKING MISTAKES

FAQ: But I can’t “stand up straight”! I’ve tried, but I keep
falling back down! All the stuff in my life is crushing me!
FAQ: But I keep on messing up! Maybe I should just give
up. I’ve tried so many times to do the right thing, and I
keep on failing!
FAQ: Fresh start? I’ve tried so many times, and it
doesn’t work! Why shouldn’t I just give up?
We start our day by saying Modeh Ani. In the siddur, we explained it
in the following way:
‫ — מודה אני‬Thank You, Hashem, for giving my neshamah back to
me (which was a merciful thing to do, since I have not yet lived
up to my full potential, and therefore may not really deserve
that); ‫ — רבה אמונתך‬Your belief in me is very great! (You believe
I am important; my struggles are important; and You believe
I can climb up to the next rung — that is why You woke me up.
Thank You!)
The very fact that Hashem woke us up shows that He believes in each
and every one of us! He does believe that you can push forward, and
He does believe that you can climb to the next level in your avodas
Hashem! You just need to believe in yourself as much as Hashem
believes in you! (Please read that again!)
“But I keep on failing!” “I keep on making mistakes!” Right, me too.
So does everyone!
That is the process of growth. The pasuk (Mishlei 24:16) says clearly,
“Sheva yipol tzaddik v’kam — The tzaddik falls down seven times and
gets back up.” Tzaddikim fall. What makes someone a tzaddik is not
that they don’t fall, but rather that they get up!
Rav Hutner takes it further. He explains that making mistakes is an
integral part of the growth process! Rav Hutner says that the true
meaning of the pasuk “Sheva yipol tzaddik v’kam” is that the only way
to become a tzaddik is through the process of making mistakes! It is

251 Appendix 3

not simply that tzaddikim are human and thus make mistakes. The
only way they become tzaddikim is by exercising their bechirah (free
choice), and perforce sometimes making mistakes along the way
until they figure it all out! The very struggle with our yetzer hara, the
stumblings, tactical retreats, crashes, and self-disappointment all are
a necessary, normal, and healthy part of the growth process!
Listen to one of my very favorite vorts: We know that the thirtynine prohibited melachos (creative work) of Shabbos come from the
Mishkan, the traveling Tabernacle. Whatever was done to create
a Mishkan we do not do on Shabbos since we do not create on
Shabbos. One of the melachos is mochek, erasing. Where was erasing
used in creating the Mishkan? When they made the kerashim, the
boards, they needed to write symbols on them so that when they
were reassembled at each stop, they would know where each board
went (each board had to be in the exact same position each time they
erected the Mishkan). What if you wrote the wrong symbol? You
had to erase it, and thus the prohibition of erasing on Shabbos. But
why should that be considered a meleches machsheves, creative work?
You only erased because you made a mistake! The answer is that you
cannot build a Mishkan without making mistakes! Making mistakes
is part of our growth process. As we said, a flat smooth straight line
on your EKG means you are dead.
So assuming you woke up, that means that Hashem believes you have
a crucial role to play and can conquer whatever He set in your way. If
you fail, get back up; it means you are a tzaddik!

Appendix 4 252

APPENDIX 4
AM SEGULAH/AM NIVCHAR —
THE CHOSEN PEOPLE

FAQ: But I do NOT understand the Torah, and to be
honest, I am NOT happy that I was stuck being part of
the chosen people! What’s so great about it?
FAQ: Chosen people? Doesn’t this brachah sound
racist? I mean, I know a lot of really nice non-Jews — and
some “not-so-great” Jews! Is there really such a
difference?
Simply put, the fact that Hashem took us out of Egypt, and three
million Jews all heard Him speak at Har Sinai — choosing us as His
nation and giving us His Torah and a mission to the world — that
is a historical reality! And just like you had no choice as to your
complexion, height, or parents, you had no choice whether to be born
as part of Klal Yisrael or not. You get what you get — deal with it!
Keep in mind that although this mission does come with a lot of
responsibility and work, it is a beautiful way of life (full of depth,
beauty, and meaning), and the Torah promises great reward. At my
stage in life, since I have experienced more than you (and studied
more Torah sources), I can see that! But it is possible that for many
readers, at this moment there are more parts of Torah that don’t make
sense to you than those that do make sense! That does make it hard.
And perhaps, unfortunately, your personal experience of Yiddishkeit
has not been sweet for all sorts of reasons. That definitely makes it
hard. But Matan Torah and us being Hashem’s special treasure is a
reality. (Proving that is not hard at all, but that topic is not for here
but for another time and another book. But there are resources out
there that cover this topic fully, and a simple look at the miraculous
survival of Bnei Yisrael is proof itself!). So if you were born into it,
then you need to get with the program, do your best, and hang on
until you learn, experience more, and access the sweetness, which I
assure you is there!
What does it mean to be Hashem’s am segulah, His special treasure?
Obviously, it doesn’t mean that we are automatically nicer and better

253 Appendix 4

people than everyone else. Unfortunately, we all see that this is not
true! Rather, it means that Hashem has given us an extra bit of
“Godliness,” an extra kedushah and a special neshamah that has the
potential to allow us to get closer to Hashem than someone who does
not have this segulah. But it is only potential. It’s up to you whether
you get in touch with that extra kedushah and develop it or not.
Who has this special neshamah with this segulah? Anyone born
from a Jewish mother is born with it and thus automatically has
the responsibilities that come with that, as we said above. As well,
anyone is welcome to join the chosen people and can also get that
special neshamah and that segulah! When non-Jews convert, they
get that special neshamah, which is why the Gemara views geirim as
having been born anew. But they must go through the process and
need to agree to keep all the responsibilities (613 mitzvos) that come
with that special neshamah and unique mission to the world.

Appendix 5 254

APPENDIX 5
BECHIRAH CHOFSHIS — FREE WILL

FAQ: Which “steps” does Hashem decide and set for us,
and which do we choose on our own?
FAQ: If in the end “only the plan of Hashem will prevail,”
no matter what humans think and plan, where does our
free will fit in?
We can’t fully explain here the sources, details, and complexity of our
free will and Hashem’s hashgachah and providence. There are entire
books on the subject — and this is just an appendix to a siddur! Still,
let us give the very basis in a nutshell.
As the Rambam (Chapter 8 of Shemoneh Perakim) explains: “Ha’kol
b’yedei Shamayim chutz mi’yiras Shamayim — Everything is controlled
by Hashem except for decisions that relate to our fear of Heaven,”
i.e., mitzvah or aveirah choices. Meaning, He controls our reality and
sets up our situations. He decided that I will face a choice of whether
to give a hundred dollars or two hundred dollars to tzedakah, and
not the choice of whether to give one million or two million dollars
to tzedakah (since He did not give me that much money). But He will
not make my choice of which of those two amounts to give or if I will
give any tzedakah at all.
Sometimes, He will control our “minor” decisions that have no moral
weight of right or wrong. There are two different streets you can take
to get home. Taking Street A or Street B is not a right/wrong, good/
bad moral decision. Hashem may thus intervene in your decision
process because He needs you on Street A. But whether you speed,
whether you pick up the person on the corner who needs a ride, and
what music you listen to as you drive — all those are right/wrong,
good/bad yiras Shamayim moral decisions, and those you decide.
(“But the reality He gave me, and therefore the choices I face, are
harder and less fun than the reality and choices of my friend! That’s
not fair!” Good point! See Appendix 2.)

255 Appendix 5

Hopefully, you will make the correct decisions, and by doing that
you will help move Hashem’s master plan of “l’sakein olam b’malchus
Shakkai,” that the entire world will accept Hashem’s dominion,
forward. This will hasten the arrival of Mashiach (and earn you
reward!). But unfortunately, we don’t always make the correct
decisions (as we see that Mashiach has not yet arrived!), and then
Hashem has to readjust how things play out. Perhaps your neshamah
will have to come back as a gilgul to fix things up. But in the end,
taking all six thousand years into account (and do remember that the
true reward for our good deeds is in Olam Haba anyway), it will all
work out, and “atzas Hashem hi sakum,” Hashem’s plan will prevail.
(See the amazing mashal in Appendix 2 that relates to this.)
I want to stress that this is a very simplistic overview of a very
complex topic. If this is something you really want to understand,
there are many good books that discuss it from all different
perspectives.

Appendix 6

APPENDIX 6
SIYATA D’SHMAYA — ASKING
FOR DIVINE ASSISTANCE

FAQ: We say, “Do not allow the yetzer hara to get the
better of us” and, “Compel our creative powers to be
used to serve You.” But I thought we had free will? This
sounds like we are asking Hashem to eliminate our free
will choices!
As we said above (in the introduction to Appendix 1) regarding mazal
elyon and mazal tachton (if you skipped that, go back; otherwise, the
following will make no sense), Hashem can create situations in our
lives (if we are deserving) where it is easier for us to be successful in
fulfilling our mazal elyon mission. He can also make it harder! (See
also Appendix 5 regarding Hashem creating our life situations.)
By this request we are showing Hashem that we honestly do want to
fulfill our mission, and are asking that He set up the reality of our day
such that it will be relatively easy for us to decide to do the correct
thing and that we will not be in situations where we will be tempted
to make bad decisions. But in the end, any good/bad or right/wrong
decision we make is completely up to our free will; see Appendix 5.

256

257 Appendix 7

APPENDIX 7
ALL THAT HASHEM DOES IS GOOD
FAQ: “Do for us good acts of kindness.” What does that
mean? Are there “bad” acts of kindness?
All that Hashem does for us is good for us. But there are sweet, tasty
medicines and bitter medicines! They are both good for us, but we
certainly would prefer the sweet one! Similarly, we ask Hashem that
the acts of kindness He does for us should be sweet and pleasant,
rather than harsh and bitter. Similarly, on Rosh Hashanah, we wish
everyone, “Shanah tovah u’mesukah — A good and sweet year.” Why
“sweet”? Because of course it will be a good year; all that Hashem
does is good! But we wish our friends that the good Hashem does for
them will be a “sweet good.”

Appendix 8

APPENDIX 8
OUR MATERIAL WORLD

FAQ: Well, to be honest, I just want all this stuff
because … I want it! Is that so bad?
FAQ: Honestly, I don’t really care that much about my
unique mission in this world. I just daven because I need
stuff from Hashem. Is that so bad?
In the fifth chapter of Shemoneh Perakim, the Rambam describes the
ideal approach a person should take to running their lives, based on
the Mishnah: “V’chol maasecha yiheyu l’shem Shamayim — Everything
you do should be for the sake of Heaven.” In theory, everything we
do or say, everything we buy, and how we spend every minute of the
day should all be based on what will maximize our avodas Hashem. In
theory, why would we want something that will not help us in serving
Hashem and help us in fulfilling our mission? What purpose would
it serve? But the Rambam himself says that this is a very high level of
running one’s life, which is reached only by a select few and only after
many years of work. But it is important to understand the concept
and to have something to aim for.
We should put thought into why we want stuff and why we spend
money on things. Obviously, we can’t maximize our service of
Hashem if we’re tired, hungry, cranky, tense, and irritable. So we do
need some stuff to solve those issues — each in our own way and on
our own level. But even for “normal” people, acquiring things just
out of jealousy or to make others jealous of us is not healthy. And
certainly, to use our material things to create a false sense of self is
not going to help us grow.
So to sum up, it is understandable that at a certain point in our lives
we just “want stuff because I want it,” and no, that’s not so bad. But
at least during our tefillah to Hashem we should have a sense of what
we are aspiring to.

258

259 Appendix 9

APPENDIX 9
KORBANOS

FAQ: Really? Am I supposed to wake up each morning
(and on Rosh Chodesh and Yom Tov) and say to myself:
“I sooo wish I could go bring an animal sacrifice this
morning”? How are we supposed to relate to a concept
that seems to be so foreign to us?
First, let’s get rid of some misconceptions. The word “korban” does
not translate as “sacrifice” (as in giving something up), even though
in English we seem to use the term “animal sacrifices.” It also does
not translate as “offering,” as in bringing a gift or bribe to the angry
gods, as it is in the way we view avodah zarah.
Rather, korban means “to come near.” Korbanos help a person get
closer to Hashem. When we had a Beis Hamikdash, we took the
animal parts, and by placing them on the Mizbei’ach (Altar), they
became elevated. This is a personal declaration of our intentions
to elevate all the material stuff that Hashem gave us (our physical
powers, emotional skills, possessions — whatever Hashem gave
us) to a higher level, to direct it all toward serving Hashem and
accomplishing our mission here on earth. In addition, the blood
of the animal sprinkled on the Mizbei’ach serves as a reminder of
our vulnerability. This “near-death” type of experience is meant to
motivate us to continue growing spiritually and getting as close to
Hashem as humanly possible while we’re still on this earth — which
is not forever!
To emphasize the point that korbanos are to bring us closer to
Hashem rather than to “appease an angry god,” it is interesting to
note that the name of Hashem used in connection with korbanos is
specifically His four-letter name that we do not pronounce, which is
His attribute of love and mercy. The name Elokim (which represents
His trait of strict judgment) only appears five times in the whole of
Sefer Vayikra, which primarily discusses the korbanos, and always in
the context of other nations, not Bnei Yisrael!

Appendix 9

The above is a simple overview of the concept of korbanos. In truth,
it is much more complex than that. Really, we would need to look at
each detail of each different korban, since each korban was brought
for different things. The choice of animal and details of the avodah
reflected the concept and essence of that particular korban and
symbolized to the person bringing the korban specifically what he
should be focusing on! When we daven and ask Hashem to bring back
the korbanos, really what we are saying is the following:
Hashem, I know that there are very deep meanings in each and
every korban, and that they serve to help me cleanse my sins and
elevate my life. But in this galus we are so insensitive to kedushah
that I simply cannot understand that! Please rebuild the Beis
Hamikdash, give us back the ability to “smell” the beautiful
aroma of kedushah and understand the power and meaning of the
korbanos. And I am sure that when that happens, we will bring all
the korbanos with joy!
One last but very important point. All mitzvos have an inherent,
underlying, and deep-seated reality and kedushah in their own
right. And that goes way beyond any symbolism or explanation that
we may give. Here we have mentioned some of the most common,
basic “explanations” of korbanos. But in the end, if Hashem said that
something is crucial for our neshamos, then it is! He created our
neshamos and knows what is best for them, even if we have a hard
time understanding that!

260

261 Appendix 10

APPENDIX 10
MAKING HASHEM “HAPPY”

FAQ: What does it mean that Hashem is “‫הבוחר בשירי‬
‫ — זמרה‬takes pleasure in songs of praise”? Hashem is not
human; what does it mean that He “takes pleasure”?
We always use “human terminology” when talking about Hashem
(e.g., the “Hand of Hashem”), since we are human and cannot relate
to Hashem in any other way. In this particular instance, the meaning
is the following: Hashem creates challenges for us to overcome so
that we can accomplish our mission and earn our reward. He wants
us to pass those tests by overcoming those challenges! One of the
biggest challenges we have in life is praising Hashem under all
circumstances, even when we are dealing with things that are hard
for us to understand. That’s a tough challenge! When we do praise
Hashem no matter what, we pass that test. Thus Hashem “takes
pleasure,” meaning He is happy with us when we do praise Him even
when it’s hard for us, since that shows that we have succeeded in
dealing with that difficult challenge.

Appendix 11

262

APPENDIX 11
BEN TORAH/BAS YISRAEL
FAQ: Is it so bad to be a woman that men thank
Hashem that “You did not create me a woman”? And
were men NOT created “according to Your will”?
FAQ: Women are also “committed to putting Torah
into practice,” so how come they don’t put on tallis and
tefillin?
FAQ: The general custom is that women do not say
Tachanun. But why? Why the differences in tefillah (and
practices) between men and women?

I think everyone understands that this is not the place for a full
discussion on this topic. Entire books have been written on this topic,
whereas this is simply an appendix to a siddur! More importantly, a
lot depends on your personal experience and your family background.
Of all the FAQs here, it is this one that is most likely to be understood
differently by different people — just as things look different to two
people, one of whom is wearing green-tinted glasses and the other
yellow-tinted glasses!
In addition, a lot depends on what you’ve already heard about this
topic. Assuming you’ve had a good, standard yeshiva/Bais Yaakov
education, you’ll have heard all the many varied and different
explanations taught to boys and girls. If they resonated with you and
you were satisfied with the answers, then great! You don’t need me.
And if it all still bothers you, then one paragraph (even written by
me!) may not help!
“Rabbi Feigenbaum, are you trying to just duck and avoid this? Is this
going to be another one of those classic, ‘Oh, it’s very deep and we’re
not worthy of understanding … ’ non-answer answers? Are you going
to hide behind a lot of words in the hope that we don’t catch on that
you have no answer? That is not why I bought this siddur!”

Introduction

263 Appendix 11

Now Let’s Get
to the FAQs

Absolutely not! I don’t do that. But I am serious about what I said
above that this particular topic is more suited for a real and complex
discussion, and not for an appendix to a siddur.
“So why did you include the question?” To show you that it is a good
question! Chazal were careful how they worded things, so if they
worded two brachos the way they did, there was a reason or many
reasons, and the topic deserves study. And most certainly there are
reasons why certain mitzvos and practices are different for men
and women.
“So you’re not going to say anything on this topic?” I’m going to
present the approach that resonates with me. Of all the ideas and
answers I have read and heard, this one works for me. But I am me,
and you are not. And I don’t know what glasses you are wearing. I
know what I’m writing, but I don’t know what you’re reading! If this
helps, great! If not, also great! As I explained above, there are many
approaches, all true on different levels, and you need the one that
fits who you are.
I know that was really a long introduction to the following short
answer! But in this case, the introduction was more important than
the answer itself.
Here we go: We are all Hashem’s children. As children, all are beloved
in the eyes of Hashem. But to accomplish our great mission in the
world (until Mashiach comes), each “kid” is given a different job.
Hashem needs a lot of different positions filled. We need kings,
judges, Kohanim, and Leviim. We had twelve Shevatim — each had
its own job, own flag, and own colored stone on the Choshen. And we
need men and women. In general, men connect to kedushah better
when that connection is structured and involves doing things within
specific guidelines. That structure and defined “doing” of mitzvos,
at specific times with specific objects, helps them focus on the
spiritual and not get sidetracked from their mission. Women have
a more intuitive, inner sense of kedushah, which in a certain way is
more in line with Hashem’s Will (intuitive, flowing avodas Hashem),
since that’s really how things should have been. Women, therefore,
don’t need all that structure and all those time constraints. These
could actually hamper them by allowing the external aspects of the
mitzvah to give them a false sense of kedushah, which is a risk men
run all the time.
So if we compare those two ways of connecting to kedushah
(men — defined and structured, as compared to women —
intuitive and flowing), we discover the following: Practically, in this
very material world, it is easier to have defined times and defined
mitzvos and practices to force us to stop and connect to kedushah. Not
having that is a harder challenge. Thus, when looking at it practically,
men are happy that they were not given that more difficult challenge,

Appendix 11

and thus they say “she’lo asani ishah.” Women, however, cannot say
that they are happy they are not men, because that would make it
sound like they are happy they have less mitzvos and are looking to
get out of serving Hashem — which is completely not true!
In truth, women were made closer in line with God’s Will (albeit, with
a role that involves more difficult challenges), since intuitive avodas
Hashem is in many areas actually the ideal. This is what women
are thanking Hashem for when they say “she’asani kirtzono.” They
therefore also have less specific and defined mitzvos and practices.
But as far as closeness to Hashem, everyone is judged as to how
well they did their job! You may prefer some other job, and maybe
at different times different jobs seem more glamorous or more
prestigious. But as we said at the beginning of this answer, we are all
Hashem’s children. As children, all are beloved in the eyes of Hashem.
And you will be judged on your mission and on how well you did your
job — not how well you did someone else’s!
I hope that made sense and helped. If yes, great. If that made things
worse, I apologize! As I wrote earlier: If these answers raise even
more questions, great! Go find books on the subject, ask your rebbeim
and teachers, or email me at rabbiyfeigenbaum@gmail.com, and I will
try to clarify things.

265 Appendix 12

APPENDIX 12
HALACHOS
This is a very simplified overview of the most common
mistakes people make, and a very basic “Uh-oh, what
do I do now?” guide. This will not cover all possibilities,
and of course in certain areas, there are multiple
opinions. Preferably, you should consult your posek for
detailed guidance of what to do when such issues arise.

12:1
‫ָכ ֵרנּו ְל ַח ִיּים‬
ְ‫ז‬

12:1
‫ִמי ָכמֹוָך‬
‫ָאב ָה ַר ֲח ִמים‬

12:1
‫ַה ֶּמ ֶלְך ַה ָּקדֹוׁש‬

If you did not yet say Hashem’s name in the brachah of ‫מגן אברהם‬, go
back and say ‫ זכרנו לחיים‬and continue from there. If you already said
Hashem’s name, do not go back.
If you did not yet say Hashem’s name in the brachah of ‫מחיה המתים‬,
go back and say ‫ מי כמוך‬and continue from there. If you already said
Hashem’s name, do not go back.
If you made a mistake and said ‫ל הקדוש‬-‫הא‬, then it depends: If you
remembered immediately (the time it takes to say the phrase “‫שלום‬
‫)”עליך רבי‬, then quickly correct yourself and say ‫המלך הקדוש‬. But if
more time has passed, you must start your Shemoneh Esrei over again
from the beginning. If you are not sure what you said, we assume you
said what is normally said during the year, and thus you must go back
and start Shemoneh Esrei over again.

12:2
‫טֹובים‬
ִ ‫ּוכתֹב ְל ַח ִיּים‬
ְ

If you did not yet say Hashem’s name in the brachah of ‫הטוב שמך‬, go
back and say ‫ וכתוב לחיים טובים‬and continue from there. If you already
said Hashem’s name, do not go back.

12:2
‫ְּב ֵס ֶפר ַח ִיּים‬

If you did not yet say Hashem’s name in the brachah of ‫המברך את עמו‬
‫ישראל‬, go back and say ‫ בספר חיים‬and continue from there. If you
already said Hashem’s name, do not go back.

Appendix 12

In the winter: It depends. If you remembered that you skipped ‫משיב‬
‫ הרוח‬before you said ‫ונאמן‬, say it then. If you have already said ‫ונאמן‬
but not yet said the name of Hashem in the brachah of ‫מחיה המתים‬,
say ‫ משיב הרוח‬and start ‫ ונאמן‬again. If you have already said the name
of Hashem, finish the brachah and immediately say ‫משיב הרוח‬. Even if
you finished the brachah, if you have not yet said the words ‫אתה קדוש‬
of the next brachah, say ‫ משיב הרוח‬immediately and continue. But if
you did not remember until after you started ‫אתה קדוש‬, you must
start Shemoneh Esrei over again.

266

12:3
‫רּוח‬
ַ ‫ַמ ִּׁשיב ָה‬
/‫ּומֹוריד ַה ֶּג ֶׁשם‬
ִ
‫מֹוריד ַה ָּטל‬
ִ

But — if you were accustomed to saying ‫ מוריד הטל‬all summer (as does
Nusach Sephard, as well as Nusach Ashkenaz in Israel), then if you
forgot to say ‫ משיב הרוח‬during the winter, you do not need to go back,
since you said ‫מוריד הטל‬.
In the summer: If by accident you said ‫ — משיב הרוח‬if it is before the
brachah, then go back and start ‫ מכלכל חיים‬again. If you already said
the brachah, you must start Shemoneh Esrei over again.
If you are not sure what you said: Within thirty days of when Pesach
began, we assume you said what you had been saying all winter, and
within thirty days of Shemini Atzeres we assume you said whatever
you had been saying all summer!
In the winter: It depends. If you remembered that you forgot to say
‫ ותן טל ומטר‬before you said the name of Hashem in the brachah of ‫מברך‬
‫השנים‬, say ‫ ותן טל ומטר‬then and continue. If you have already said the
name of Hashem, add ‫ ותן טל ומטר‬in the brachah of ‫שמע קולנו‬. If you
already started ‫רצה‬, then you must go back to ‫ברך עלינו‬. If you already
have finished Shemoneh Esrei (by saying the words ‫)יהיו לרצון אמרי פי‬,
you must start Shemoneh Esrei over again.

12:4
/‫ְתן ְּב ָר ָכה‬
ֵ‫ו‬
‫ּומ ָטר‬
ָ ‫ְתן ַטל‬
ֵ‫ו‬
‫ִל ְב ָר ָכה‬

In the summer: If you said ‫ ותן טל ומטר‬instead of ‫ותן ברכה‬, you must
go back to ‫ברך עלינו‬. If you have already finished Shemoneh Esrei (by
saying the words ‫)יהיו לרצון אמרי פי‬, you must start Shemoneh Esrei
over again.
If you are not sure what you said: Within thirty days of when the
change was supposed to take place, we assume you said what you
were saying before that.
If you made a mistake and did not make this change to the brachah,
then it depends: If you remembered immediately (the time it takes to
say the phrase “‫)”שלום עליך רבי‬, then quickly correct yourself and say
‫המלך המשפט‬. But if more time has passed, then just continue; you do
not have to go back to fix this.

12:5
‫ַה ֶּמ ֶלְך ַה ִמ ְׁש ָּפט‬

267 Appendix 12

12:6
‫ַע ֶלה ְו ָיבֹא‬
ֲ‫י‬
in davening

On Rosh Chodesh, Maariv: If you forgot to say ‫יעלה ויבא‬, you do not
need to go back.
On Rosh Chodesh, Shacharis and Minchah: If you forgot to say ‫יעלה‬
‫ויבא‬, then it depends: If you remembered before you said the name of
Hashem in the brachah of ‫המחזיר שכינתו לציון‬, then go back to ‫יעלה ויבא‬.
If you finished the brachah but did not yet say ‫מודים‬, then say it there
and continue with ‫מודים‬. If you started ‫מודים‬, then you must go back
to ‫רצה‬, and if you have already finished Shemoneh Esrei (by saying the
words ‫)יהיו לרצון אמרי פי‬, you must start Shemoneh Esrei over again.
On Chol Hamoed: All three tefillos have the same laws as Rosh
Chodesh Shacharis above.
If you are not sure if you said ‫יעלה ויבא‬, then we assume you did not!

12:7
‫ּסים‬
ִּ ‫ַעל ַה ִנ‬
in davening

If you forgot to say ‫ על הנסים‬but remembered before you said the
name of Hashem in the brachah of ‫ולך נאה להודות‬, then go back to ‫על‬
‫ הנסים‬and continue from there. But if you already said the name of
Hashem in the brachah of ‫ולך נאה להודות‬, do not go back.

12:8
‫ּסים‬
ִּ ‫ַעל ַה ִנ‬
in ‫ברכת המזון‬

If you forgot to say ‫ על הנסים‬but remembered before you said the
name of Hashem in the brachah of ‫על הארץ ועל המזון‬, go back to ‫על‬
‫ הנסים‬and continue from there. But if you already said the name of
Hashem in the brachah of ‫על הארץ ועל המזון‬, do not go back.

12:8 ‫רצה‬

If you forgot to say ‫ רצה‬at the first two Shabbos meals, you must
start over.

12:8
‫ַע ֶלה ְו ָיבֹא‬
ֲ‫י‬
in ‫ברכת המזון‬

On Rosh Chodesh: If you forgot to say ‫יעלה ויבא‬, you do not need
to go back.
On Yom Tov: If you forgot to say ‫ יעלה ויבא‬at the two Yom Tov meals,
you must start over.
On Chol Hamoed: If you forgot to say ‫יעלה ויבא‬, you do not need
to go back.
If you are not sure if you said ‫יעלה ויבא‬, then we assume you did not!

12:9
When ‫ תחנון‬is
omitted

There are many different customs in different communities as to
when Tachanun is omitted during Shacharis and/or Minchah. Here we
will present the most basic guidelines of when Tachanun is not said,
but one should follow the custom of their shul and ask their rav for
personal guidance in this area.
• After sunset
• In the house of an Avel

Appendix 12

• If a chassan is in shul, the entire week of his sheva brachos (There
are different opinions regarding the wedding day itself.)
• In a shul where a bris is being held that day
• If one of the baalei ha’bris (father of the baby, mohel, sandek)
is in shul
• Erev Rosh Hashanah
• Erev Yom Kippur
Tachanun is not recited on the following days nor is it recited at
Minchah preceding these days:

ABOUT THE AUTHOR
Rabbi Yitzchak Feigenbaum has been educating adolescents
and young adults for over forty years. Born in Cleveland, Ohio,
he spent many years studying in Eretz Yisrael, and received
his semichah from Hagaon Harav Beinush Finkel, zt”l, Rosh
Yeshivas Mir, Yerushalayim.
Rabbi Feigenbaum was the first Mashgiach of Aish HaTorah
in Yerushalayim when it was founded, and in 1978 cofounded
Yeshivat Darche Noam/David Shapell College of Jewish
Studies. During that time, he authored Understanding the
Talmud: A Systematic Guide to Talmudic Logic and Methodology
(Feldheim, 1987). In 1987, he and his family moved to
Toronto, where he taught in a variety of high schools for both
boys and girls; lectured for Aish HaTorah, Ohr Somayach, and
NCSY; and served as the scholar in residence for schools and
shuls across North America.
In September 2000, Rabbi Feigenbaum became the founding
principal of Tiferes Bais Yaakov High School for girls. Besides
serving as principal, he personally taught Chumash and his
unique hashkafah course. Over the past two decades, he has
mentored teachers and principals, served as an educational
consultant for schools worldwide, counseled young people
across the world, and lectured extensively in schools, shuls,
and camps in North America, England, Eretz Yisrael, and the
former Soviet Union.
Upon his retirement from Tiferes Bais Yaakov, Rabbi
Feigenbaum has drawn on his extensive experience as a
classroom teacher, principal, speaker, counselor, and mentor
to create an innovative and all-encompassing Teachers

Training Course, a Parenting Course, as well as his weekly twominute parashah series, “Instant Insights.” All these, as well as
a plethora of his shiurim on a variety of topics, are available
at his website: www.rabbiyfeigenbaum.com. In addition, his
podcast “Instant Insight with Rabbi Feigenbaum” is available
on your favorite podcast app. He may be contacted directly at
rabbiyfeigenbaum@gmail.com or through his website.
